EMBASSY OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
Rome, Italy

July 18, 1946

No. 3823
OCD
Subject: Tranamitting Itemized List of Contents of Arohives of
Former Japanese Embassy to Italy
CORRELATION
-5-53
Fiée alcorcoron
JUL51946
DeR
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington, D. C.
Sir:
FC-SPD-JK-WE
894.414/7-1846
With reference to Department's circular airgram of Jamuary 31,
1946, 2:30 p.m., Embassy's airgram no. A-272, of March 16, 1946, and
Embassy's despatch no. 3364, of April 17, 1946, I have the honor to
report that the team of American soldiers (Niseis) who have been
examining and cataloging the archives of the former Japanese Embassy
to Italy has completed its work and has prepared a complete itemised
list of the contents of these archives for presentation to and exm-
ination by the Four Powers charged with liquidating Japanese Embassy
property and archives in Rome. One copy of the list in question is
transmitted herewith.
After preparing an original list of the contents of the archives
by the process of listing each item as it was examined - which
resulted in a list of over 1500 items numbered at random - the Nisei
team was requested to break dowm this list into general subjects of
interest, grouping the various items under such titles as political,
economic, records, etc. The list transmitted herewith is the broken-
towm list with the original numbers assigned to each item retained in
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
the lefthand margin in order to facilitate identification of any
REPONTING dERVICES
DIVISION OF FOREIGN
particular item in the disordered mass of archives.
AUG 1 - 1946
In this connection it should be explained that the Nisei team
found the arohives strewn about helter-skelter in several rooms and
halls. Since the team is composed of enlisted men with no experience
in sorting, analyzing or cataloging archives, it carried out its
assigned task by the simple expedient of tackling each sector of the
mass of papers and examining, numbering and listing each item as it
came to it.
According to the six members of the Nisei team, as well as from
a cursory perusal of the items in the list, the archives contain
nothing of particular interest, information, or significance. Most
of the archives, except for "Records",are printed material and unless
atherwise indicated are in the Japanase language. Nevertheless, a
894.414
24
FST W STAR
SEP 1 7 1946

of the original arehkves, bearing in mind the recommendatien of the
four Embassies that all prepaganda material be destreyed exsept for one
copy of each item to be made available to each Embassy.
Because of the imminent redeployment of most of the members of the
Nisei team, it is respectfully requested that any requests fer miere-
filming be telegraphed to this Embassy as soon as possible so that some
of the members of the team may locate and extract any desired items
from the mass of papers.
Respectfully yours,

ENCLOSURE
PROVISIONS FOR THE COORDINATED EXPLOITATION OF JAPANEBE
INTELLIGENCE TARGETS OF INTERNATIONAL CONCERN
Report by the
State-War-Navy Coordinating Subcommittee for the Far East
THE PROBLEM
1. To formulate a U. S. policy for international coordina
tion in the exploitation of intelligence targets in Japan and
areas in the Far East and Pacific formerly occupied by the
Japanese and now under American command.
FACTS BEARING ON THE PROBLEM
2. See Appendix "A".
DISCUSSION
3. See Appendix "B".
CONCLUSIONS
Coordination of and action upon requests received from
other nations for intelligence and intelligence material under
United Suates control in Japan or in areas in the Far East and
Pacific formerly occupied by the Japanese and now under Amer-
ican command should be effected by CINCAFPAC or CINCPAC, each
in his respective area of authority, acting under policy guid-
ance of the State, War and Navy Departments.
5. The following rules should be considered as general
guidance in implementation of the above principle:
a.
All governments participating in the Far Eastern
Comlission are recognized as entitled to that intelligence
necessary to enable them effectively to perform their
functions vith respect to occupation and occupation poli-
cies for Japan.
b. Except when included in the definition in a above,
intelligence related to operatlons during hostilities or
SWN00 216/1
-12-
Enclosure

and technology, should be provided when requested, to
governments which actively participated in the war
against Japan, subject to the provisions of subparagraph
e below:
C. Intelligence requested by a government which
actively participatod in tho war against Japan to enable
it to administer its territories or liberated areas in
the Far East or to protect the property and rights of
its citizens therein should be provided, subject to the
provisions of subparagraph e below.
d, Political, oommercial, geographic or other intelli-

c. Intelligence which, in the opinion of the Amer an

=== Page 142 === (confidence 0.98)
S E C R ET.
APPENDIX "A"
FACTS BEARING ON THE PROBLEM
1. This paper concerns the question of sharing with o
nations the results of United States exploitation of Japaneso
intelligence targets. It was raised specifically by a Britich
proposal for the establishment in Washington of a "combined
oxecutive organization" with funotions similar to those of the
former Combined Intelligence Objeotives Subcommittee in London.
This latter organization served to coordinate the interests of
British and U. S. governmental departments for intelligenoe of
military or political significance in areas liberated from
German control by SHAEF.
2. The Joint Chiefs of Staff in JCS 1538 approved the
principle that international coordination of intelligence
targets in the Far East should be effected by the supreme
authority in each occupied and liberated area. The Commander-
in-Chief, U. S. Army Forces, Pacific was notified of this
decision and authorized to permit other Allied nations to have
access to intelligence or intelligence targets essentially re-
lated to (1) that nation's operations during hostilities, (2)
its responsibilities in connection with the occupation and
control of Japan or other Far Eastern area, or (3) its govern-
ment of liberated territories, subject to appropriate reciprocal
action by that nation with respect to intelligence or intelli-
gence targets under its control.
3. The British were informed of the substance of the above
decision by the United States Members of the Combined Intelli-
gence Committee and the suggestion was made that British target
lists and plans for target exploitation with respect to Japan
be submitted to tho Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
tapou h the senior British officer with him.

=== Page 143 === (confidence 0.96)
2
S E C R E T
4. Further Joint Chiefs of Staff action on J0s 1558
to request the State-War-Navy Coordinating Committee to
formulate a policy for international coordination in the ox
ploitation of intelligence targets in the Far East.
5. The problem was referred by the State-War-Navy Co-
ordinating Committee to the State-War-Navy Coordinating
Subcommittee for the Far East on 26 October 1945. The Sub-
committee subsequently (52nd SFE Meeting, 20 November 1945)
limited the scope of the paper to international coordination
in the exploitation of Japanese intelligence targets in areas
under American occupation.

=== Page 144 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
APPENDIX "B"
DISCUSSION
1. The JCs decision assigning responsibility for int
national coordination to CINCAFPAC is adequate to permit
coordination of local intelligence, collection and prooure
ment operations in areas of CINCAFPAC responsibility, but
should be extended to include similar directives to CINOPAd.
2.
In addition it is considered that further definition
of the types of intelligence to be supplied other governmente
is necessary to assure the ability of the United States to
meet its international commitments with respect to the ooou-
pation of Japan and at the same time to enjoy such intelligenge
advantages as may properly be derived from the major role
played by U. S. forces in her defeat.
3. It is felt that the above mentioned commitments do
not necessarily obligate the United States to share all in-
telligence exploited equally with the other occupying powers
or powers represented on the Far Eastern Commission.
Certain
Japanese intelligence must be safeguarded for United States
security.
Sharing with other nations intelligence which the
Japanese had collected against the United States and certain
other countries, would be contrary to U. S. interests.
4.
The advantages mentioned in paragraph 2 above and the
concept of United States control over intelligence in Japan
can be justified on the grounds of initial United States oc-
cupation of Japan. Unless other governments making up the
group of Allied Powers for whom SCAP accepted surrender of all
Japanese forces, share with all the Allied Powers, Japanese
intelligence from the areas they occupied after surrender, the
United States has no obligation to share all intelligenee from
Japan.

=== Page 204 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure #1 to Despatoh 14,304,
Amerioan Emb assy, Santiago, Chile,
AIR MAIL
August 8, 1946.
MEMORANDUM
Santiago, Chile,
August 2, 1946.
SUBJECT :
Conversation between Mr. Miohael L. MUKHIN, First
Seoretary of the Soviet Embassy, Santiago, and
ür. Reed.
Friday, August 2, Miss Meletine, Secretary of the Soviet
Ambassador, called me to ask for an appointment for Mr. Mukhin,
First Secretary of the Soviet Embassy in Santiago.
At 4 o'clook that afternoon, Mr. Mukhin called, accompanied
by Miss Meletina, who explained that she had come to interpret
for Mr. Mukhin since he did not speak English.
Miss Meletina is about 35 years of age, of rather plain,
Slavio appearance, but vivacious and quite attraotive. Her
English is fluent although with an acoent, and her complete
facility of expression might indicate she has resided in an
English speaking country. Her personality and attitude were in
direot oontrast to Mukhin's, who, during our half-hour conversa-
tion, rarely looked up from the floor or away from Miss Meletina,
to whom he addressed all the conversation. Mukhin is a well-built,
average height man, about 45 years of age, and wears excessively
thick glasses. He is blond and not displeasing in appearance.
He seemed to be of a melanoholy nature, and only smiled slightly
on one or two oocasions when something amusing came up in the con-
versation. He is well trained in his own government's point of
view and procedure, but diffident and even shy in his relations
with strangers. The purpose of his visit was to discuss with
me the status of Japanese archives and property in Chile.
Mukhin said he wanted to know more about the Japanese
archives mentioned in Ambassador Bowers' note of June 24, 1946
to the Soviet Ambassador. I told him the original copy of
Minister Yamagata's Diary, as wall as other doouments, were
at present in the hands of the Chinese Lagation, and, of course,
available to the Soviet Embassy for perusal. I said a member
of the Chinese Legation had translated into English the Diary
and that we had a copy of this translation whioh he could borrow
if he wished to do so. To this he replied that the dooument was,
no doubt, very important, and that he would like very muoh to
borrow it.
He then asked what had become of the Japanese Legation
residence and chancery buildings, and I explained the arrange-
ment whioh had been made with the Swedish Logation, also set
forth in an enolosure to the above mentioned Note. It was soon
evident that he was under the misapprehension that the Japanese
Government had owmed a residenoe in Santiago, and therefore wanted
to know what had become of it. He was further bewildered when
I told him that the Chinese Legation was now established in
those quarters. After a number of questions on his part, I made
it olear that the Japanese had owmed no property here, and that
the former Japanese Legation residenoe had been leased from
Chileans who now had sold it to the Chinese.
Mukhin

=== Page 205 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure #1 to Despatoh 14,304,
American Embassy, Santiago, Chile,
AIR MAIL
August 8, 1946.
-2-
Mukhin then asked in whose name the Japanese funds had been
placed. I stated that they were placed in an account under the
name of "Speoial Funds Account of the Embassy of the United States
of Amerioa", likewise mentioned in our Note under reference. He
then asked what objeotion there would be to selling the house-
hold effects of the former Japanese Legation and plaoing the
proceeds in a joint account of the Four Allied Powers. I replied
that if the Soviet Embassy desired to request that a meeting of
the representatives of the Allied Powers be called to disouss
this question, I would be very glad to transmit the message; and
as to the placing of the funds in a joint account, that was also
a matter which could be brought up at such a meeting. I remarked
that as Mr. Mukhin was aware, the reason the Japanese funds
account had been placed in the name of the American Embassy was
because the representatives of the Allied Powers in Santiago at
the time the Japanese properties were talken over, had specifically
requested Ambassador Bowers to act as their representative.
Mukhin then said he appreciated the information I had given
him, and that he wanted to feel free to call on me again to dis-
cuss further the matter of Japanese property.
HarnyOliton Need
Harry ClintonReed,
Third Seoretary of Embassy.

Forces, Pacific, on the subject, "State Department Documents in

31
No. 709, April 8, 1941; No. 761, April 13, 1941; and No. 762,

Mr. Roy Morgan, Chief, Investigative Section, International Pros-

XR-119.25

CS/V
to former Foreign Minister Matsuoka's affairs in Moscow. Subsequent
x15-124.946

Japanese Military Police, possibly assisted by an agent or agents in
the Consulate. It is understood that this cipher key was used for
messages of low classification and was undoubtedly employed by the
Japanese in decoding coded messages received by the Japanese Central
Telegraph Office.

Enclosure No. 1 to despatch no. 581 , dated August 29, 1946, from

COPY
CONEIDENTIAL
HEADQUARTERS
COUNTER INTELLIGENCE CORPS
METROPOLITAN UNIT NO. 80
APO 500
7 August 1946
SUBJECT: STATE DEPARTMENT DOCUMENTS IN POSSESSION OF JAPANESE FOREIGN
OFFICE
TO
: Commanding Officer, 44lst CIC Detachment, GHQ. AFPAC, APO 500
1. Transmitted herewith Memorandum Report, File No. TOK 8OM-S-134;
441, 750328, dated 5 August 1946, Subject: STATE DEPARTMENT DOCUMENTS
IN POSSESSION OF JAPANESE FOREIGN OFFICE. Reference is made to Lead Sheet,
44lst CIc Detachment, dated 17 June 1946, subject as above, requesting an
investigation be initiated to determine the following:
a. How the Japanese Foreign Office came into possession
of these documents.
b.
Did the Japanese break the code and if so when and other
circumstances surrounding this event.
c.
Why the Japanese Foreign Office wants these documents
returned by I.P.S.
2. Investigation disclosed that a Cipher Key was taken from the
American Consulate at Taihoku, Formosa by a member of the Japanese
Military Police. This Cipher Key was used to decipher messages of
minor inportance. Subject Documents were encoded in the same cipher
key as they were considered to be of general information. There is
no evidence that the more complicated code used for important messages
was broken by the Japanese.
The documents were kept in a complete file relating to
Matsuoka's affairs in Moscow and the Foreign Office has requested the
return of all files turned over to Allied agencies.
3. No recommendations.
4. Case closed this Headquarters.
5. Distribution:
3 Copies - CO, 44lst CIC Detachment
1 Copy - file
FOR COMMANDING OFFICER:
NORMAN E. STEWART

=== Page 214 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to despatch no. 581, dated Aagust 29, 1946, from
the United States Political Adviser for Japan, Takyo, on the subject
"State Department Doommeats in Possession of Japanese Foreign Office".
COPY
CONEIDENTIAL
TOK 80M-S-134 (2)
441-750328
5 August 1946
MEMORANDUM FOR THE OFFICER IN CHARGE:
SUbJOCT: STATE DEPARTIENT DOCUMENTS IN POSSESSION OF JAPANESE
FOREIGN OFFICE.
Pursuant to Lead Sheet, Hq., 44lst CIC Detachment, APO 500,
dated 17 June 1946, subject: STATE DEPARTMENT DOCUMENTS IN POSSESSION
OF JAPANESE FOREIGN OFFICE, the Commanding Officer, CIC Metropolitan
Unit No. 80, APO 500, directed thet an investigation be initiated
to determine the following:
a. How the Japanese Foreign Office came into possession
of these documents.
b.
Did the Japanese break the code and if so when and
other circumstances surrounding this event.
c.
Why the Japanese Foreign Office wants these docu-
ments returned by I.P.S.
SUBJECT is a series of telegrams which were sent from Steinhardt
in Moscow to Hull in Weshington during the period 8 April 194l to
16 Apri1 1941.
1. On 15 July 1946 this Agent interviewed Mr. Roy Morgan, Chief.
Investigative Section, I.P.S., at his office.
Morgan stated that the documente in question were discevered
about March of this year. The documents are a section of a complete
file relating to Matsuoka's affairs in Moscow. The Japanese Foreign
Office wants the entire file returned, not just the STATE DEPARTMENT
DOCUMENTS, because other Allied agencies request information which is
contained in this file. Other files were turned over to I.P.S. in
addition to the Matsuoka file. The Japanese Foreign Office has re-
quested the return of all files. Morgan further stated that SUBJECT
is a series of hand-written encoded telegrams, accompanied by a
Japanese translation of same.
2. On 17 July, 24 July and 31 July 1946, this Agent interviewed
a Confidential Informant who stated in substance as follows:
That prior to August 1940, a Cipher Key was taken from the
American Consulate at Taihoku, Formosa by a member of the Japanese
Military Police. It is believed that the Japanese Military Police
were helped by secret agents loceted in the Consulate. This Cipher
Key was used to decipher messages of minor importante. The docu-
ments is question were encoded in the same code system as established
in the stolen Cipher Key.
Because

=== Page 215 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
(Tokyo's despatch no. 58l, Enclosure 2.)
Because SUBJECT was considered by Steinhardt to be of general
information and of minor importance, the messages were sent to all
American Embassies, Consulates and Legations, encoded in this minor
code. The Japanese Central Telegraph Agency received the coded
telegrams in question and copied same, which were then submitted to
the 'Black Chamber' of the Japanese War Department, Navy Department,
and Foreign Office. The 'Black Chamber' was composed of highly
trained specialists whose duty was to decipher coded messages. The
decoding of the STATE DEPARTMENT DOCUMENTS was accomplished by aid
of the stolen Cipher Key and the techniques applied by members of
the Japanese War Department 'Elack Chamber'.
The more important messages sent from eny American Embassy
were encoded in a highly complicated code which has not, to the in-
formants knowledge, been broken.
Special Agent
3872
Approved:
H.W.McC.

FROM STEINHARDT, MOSCOW
TO
HULL, WASHINGTON
No. 709 April 8th
8 P.M.
STRICTLY CONFIDENTIAL FOR THE PRESIDENT, THE SECRETARY AND THE
UNDER-SECRETARY
Matsuoka and members of his steff and the Japanese Ambessador
and his staff were my guests at luncheon today. The following is a
summary of the remarks made to me by MATSUOKA in the course of an
extended and frank conversation after the luncheon:
(1) MATSUOKA said that neither RIBBENTROP nor HITLER had asked
for any commitments of any kind, and he requested me to advise the
President that he had not made or given any comitments whatsoever to
RIBBENTROP end HITLER that, as Japan had entered the Tri-partite Pact
to "preserve peace", Japan would not consider itself obligated to
declare war on the United States, were Germany to do so; but that, if
the United States were to declare war on Germany, the situation
might be different. He said that should Germany declare war on the
United States, which he does not anticipate, he hopes that the United
States would make no move in the Pacific unwillingly (?) Japan had
had an opportunity to make her position clear. Later in the con-
versation he said that it must be understood that Japan would adhere
to her obligation, under the Tripartite Pact, but emphasized that
Japan was not obligated to follow Germany in an attack upon the
United States.
He said that both HITLER and RIBBENTROP had told him that they
desired to limit the sphere of the war, and that they had no desire
to become involved in a war with the United States. They even sug-
gested that he should take steps to discourage any American propaganda
or agitations in Japan. (section one)
He said that he had expressed to RIBBENTROP and HITLER his desire
for peace, and that they had replied that they were equally anxious
to bring this about and hoped to do so this year.
MATSUOKA asserted that he had been impressed by HITLER, whom he
charactarized as a genius, and said that in none of his telks with
him had HITLER exhibited any of the excitable characteristics gener-
ally ascribed to him. He has been celm and reasonable.
He said that RIBBENTROP had expressed admiration for the way in
which the British were fighting. and voiced the opinion that the
British Empire should not be "destroyed". He said that HITLER firmly
believed that he could reduce British by submarine and aerial warfare
this spring and summer and that an invasion would not be necessary,
but that all preparations had been made for it and that would be at-
tempted, should it become necessary.
He told

=== Page 217 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
(Tokyo's despatch no. 581, Enclosure No. 3)
He told me that the Germans were fully prepared to invade the
Soviet Union but had no intention of doing so unless the Soviets
substantially reduced deliveries to Germany. He expressed the
opinion that the rumours of a German Attack on the Soviet Union had
been given out by the Germans in order to frighten the Soviets inte
maintaining deliveries.
He said that Italy was under the control of the Germans who
were very mich in evidence and that they had received express instruc-
tions not to "look down or talk down" to the Italians. Because of
the mutual personal relations between HITLER and MUSSOLINI, he was
convinced that there was no possibility of the English driving a
wedge between the two countries. He said that Italy would stand er
fall with Germany. He said that MUSSOLINI hed not given any evidence
of discouragement at his recent reverses and had expressed confidences
that Italy would shortly "stage a come-back".
MATSUOKA said that RIBBENTROP had expressed the opinion to him
that the coup d' etat in Yugoslavia had been engineered by the Soviets,
whereas he had expressed his own opinion that the British had been
responsible for this development.
(2) MATSUOKA said that HITLER and RIBBENTROP had urged him to
come to some agreement with the Soviets, and he had told them that he
was desirous of doing so, but could not "pay an excessive price".
He said that in his three and one half hour talk with MOLOTOV last
night, he had gotten nowhere, "as the Soviet demands were excessive".
He expects to see MOLOTOV again tomorrow, at which time he hopes
definitely to ascertain whether there is any possibility for agreements.
Post, he may defer his departure until the l3th and plans to go to
Leningrad tomorrow night. Repeated to Tokyo, End.(cut out)
He said that he was beginning to doubt the Soviet desire to reach
an agreement with Japan except upon its own terms, and that he was
not too sure that they could be counted upon to carry out any agree-
ment they might undertake. He said he did not see how he could con-
sent to major territorial concessions, as he did not believe that the
Japanese public would accept important concessions and he had endeavored
to make this clear to MOLOTOV last night. We said that the United
States could be indifferent to the Soviet Union but that Japan would
"either have to come to an agreement with the Soviet Union or fight".
(3) MATSUOKA indicated, as he has done on each previous occasion
that I have telked with him, his earnest desire to bring the war in
Dhina to an end, and his belief that the President could accomplish
this by intimating CHIANG-KAI SHEK that the United States would with-
hold any future assistance to China if CHIANG-KAI-SHEK refused to
accept "feir and reasonable terms". He repeatedly omphasized that, if
the President and Mr. HULL would "trust him" and assist him in bring-
ing about peace in China, everything in the Far East would be cleared
up to their satisfaction.
(4) MATSUOKA stated that because of his desire to see LENINGRAD.
his old..........(post, he may defer his departure, etc. as in part two).

FROM STEINHARDT, MOSCOW
TO
HULL, WASHINGTON.
No. 754. April 12th 1941
Strictly confidential. For the President and the Secretary.
KASE called on me this afternoon, and said that MATSUOKA desired
to meet the British Ambassador, but that, due to the fact that the
Japanese Ambassador had been offended by CRIPPS' attitude toward him
ever since his arrival in MOscow (see my despatch No. 117 of
February 28th 1941), MATSUOKA was unable to invite CRIPPS to call on
him. KASE accordingly suggested that MATSUOKA should call on me at
12:30 tomorrow, and that CRIPPS be present "by accident". He stressed
the fact that MATSUOKA did not wish the Japanese Ambassador to know
of the meeting nor the Germans or Italians and that he was desirous
that the meeting would not receive publicity.
I pointed out to him that, under the conditions existing in
MOSCOW of close and constant surveillance of Chiefs of Mission by the
Soviet authorities and Foreign Press representatives, it would be im-
possible for such a meeting to take place apparently "by accident".
and that publicity with various undesirable implications was bound
to follow. When I suggested as an alternative that I arrange to
have the British Ambassador meet MATSUOKA elsewhere, KASE demurred
on the grounds that MATSUOKA did not wish TATEKAWA to know of the
meeting, since TATEKAWA would object to his meeting CRIPPS. Toward
the close of the conversation, KASE stated that MATSUOKA intonded
to attend the MOSCOW Art Theater this evening. I stated that Mrs.
Steinhardt and I also would attend at the theater this evening, and
that the British Ambassador and Lady CRIPPS would be our only guests,
thus affording MATSUOKA an opportunity to meet the Ambassador and
talk with him during the intermission without evoking any comment.
I pointed out that such a meeting would appear to be quite casual
inasmuch as a substantial part of the Diplomatic Corps is usually
to be found at the Moscow Art Theater. Repeated to Tokyo.
FROM STEINHARDT, MOSCOW
HULL, WASHINGTON
No. 761. April 13th 1941.
Strictly confidential. For the President, the Secretary and the
under Secretary.
The meeting between MATSUOKA and CRIPPS took place last night at
the Theater, as arranged, without attracting any attention. In the
course of their conversation, MATSUOKA assured CRIPPS that Japan had
no hostile intentions against Britain or British Possessions in the
Far East. He expressed the hope that the war would not spread, and
talked in a generally conciliatory vein, CRIPPS handed him a copy of
CHURCHILL's Message, which MATSUOKA surreptitiously slipped into his
pocket without looking at it, obviously in order that it should not
be noticed that he had received a document.
As we were walking back to our seats, I asked MATSUOkA whether
he had reached an understanding with MOLOTov, to which he replied he
anticipated signing a "limited" agreement in the nature of a "Pact of
Neutrality"

Neutrality" before his departure (see my No. 1638 November 28th)
Repeated to Tokyo.
FROM STEINDHARDT, MOSCOW TO HULL. WASHINGTON.
No. 762 Apri1 13th 1941.
Strictly confidential. For the President, the Secretary and the
under-Secretary.
The MOSCOW radio this afternoon announced the signing of a
Treaty of Neutrality of the Soviet Union and Japan and a supplementary
Declaration. The essence of the Treaty is that each contracting
party will observe neutrality if the other is the object of military
action by a third party or parties. The essence of the Declaration
is a reciprocal undertaking to respect the territorial integrity and
inviolability of MANCHOUKUO and the MONGOLIAN PEOPLE'S REPUBLIC. For
the protection of our codes, the texts of the Treaty and the Declara-
tion are being sent in a separate telegram following and bearing the
next number after this.
The extravagant utterances and threats against the United States,
which have appeared in the German Press and which have been broad-
cast from Germany regarding the consequences to be expected from
MATSUOKA's visit to Berlin make it reasonable to suppose that the
Soviet-Japanese Treaty of Neutrality will be hailed by Germany as the
successful result of its efforts to bring about a Soviet-Japanese
political agreement, which would relieve Japan of the fear of an
attack by the Soviet Union should Japan become embroiled in hostil-
ities with the United States.
I believe, however, that the Treaty was brought much less by
German influence or a desire on the part of Japan to prepare itself
for eventual collaboration with Germany in hostilities with the United
States than by the fear on the part of Japan that it may become in-
volved in hostilities with the United States against its will, and
the desire on the part of the Soviet Government to prepare itself
against a possible attack by Germany.
During the early months of the Soviet-Japanese negotiations, it
was clear that the Soviet Government was not unduly anxious to enter
into a political agreement with Japan, and that it would only do so
for a high price, including substantial territorial concessions.
(Section one)
On the other hand, while the Japanese Government did not conceal
its eagerness to conclude a political agreement with the Soviet Union,
which it desired to be of as far-reaching a scope as possible, it was
not willing to pay the price demanded by the Soviet Government,
especially insofar as concerned the cession of lower SAkHALIN or even
the surrender of the Japanese concessions to NORTHERN SAkHALIN.
I believe that during the three weeks thst have intervened since
MATSUOKA's departure from MOSCOW for BERLIN, the attitude of both the
Soviet and Japanese Government has undergone a profound change. I am
of the opinion that the Soviet Government, having become convinced of
the possibility of an attack by Germany, decided to abandon its
favorable bargaining position in exchange for an assurance of Japanese
neutrality in the event of a German attack on the Soviet Union.
I likewise

=== Page 220 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
(Tokyo's despatch no. 581, Enclosure No. 3)
I likewise believe that, as a result of his talks with MUSSOLINI
and HITLER and also perhaps because of the impression made upon him
by the threats against the United States, which appeared in the German
and Italian Press in connection with his visit, MATSUOKA returned to
MOSCOW fearful that Japan might find itself maneuvered into a position
which would lead to hostilities with the United States. In this
connection, it will be recalled (see my No. 581 March 24th) that
MATSUOKA, on the first occasion of his first visit to MOSCOW pressed
the view to me that "American History indicates that it is the United
States which goes to war with other countries". In consequence, he
felt the necessity of coming to an agreement with the Soviet Govern-
ment, which would protect Japan against cooperation by the Soviet
Union with the United States in such an eventuality, and at the same
time indicating to Germany that he does not consider Japan bound under
the Tri-partite Pact to go to war with either the Soviet Union or the
United States in the event that Germany should declare war or take
offensive action against either country.
It would appear from the foregoing that both governments found
it expedient to abandon their earlier specific desire (such as terri-
torial concessions by Japan and cessation of aid to China by the Soviet
Union) rather than have the negotiations break down or be further pro-
longed, entering into a simple and limited reciprocal commitment to
remain neutral in the event that either country should become the
object of military action by a third party or parties.
MATSUOKA left this afternoon on the trans-Siberian for MANCHULI,
whence he will fly to Tokyo. STALIN was at the station to see him off,
an attention he did not show to von RIBBENTROP and which, in so far a
I am aware, is without precedent. His action was presumably designed
to lend the appearance of importance to the Treaty.
Shortly before his departure, MATSUOKA sent me the following
letter marked "confidential" handwritten by him. (section two)

"They streesed the necessity and importance of settling the

FROM STEINHARDT, MOSCOW
TO
HULL, WASHINGTON
No. 777 April 16th
Strictly confidential.
Foreign correspondents, who were present at the Trans-Siberian
Railway Station in the evening MATUSOKA left on his return journey
to Japan, state that it was apparent that most of the members of
MATSUOKA's Party and of the Japanese Embassy and of the Soviet Dele-
gation present to bid MATSUOFA farewell (with the exception of
STALIN) were somewhat intoxicated, and that the behaviour in the
station platform, while awaiting the departure of the train, can
only be described as frolicsome. There were many back slappings,
bearhugs and even kisses exchanged between various persons present,
and several rather extraordinary remarks were claimed to have been
overheard. The most interesting of these is one attributed to
STALIN in variations of the following words: "Now that the SOVIET
UNION and JAPAN have arranged their affairs, Japan will straighten
out the EAST. The Soviet Union and Germany will take care of the
MERICAnS". That something approximating the foregoing statement
actually was said has been confirmed by members of the German Embassy
who were also present.
The visit and the signing of the Treaty have of course provoked
an abundant flow of gossip in MOSCOW, including most recently the
circulation of a report to the effect that the real and hidden im-
portance of the Soviet-Japanese agreement is that it constitutes
the first step toward a full association on the part of the Soviet
Union with the Tri-partite Power in a concerted drive to enforce
their will on Europe and Asia.

CONFIDENTIAL:
AIR MAIL
Enclosure No. 1 to despatch no. 1063, dated Septem-
ber 3, 1946, entitled "Japanese Archives in the
Union of South Africa" from the American Legation,
Pretoria.
LEGATION OF THE UNITED STATES OF ANERICA
The American Legation presents its compliments
to the Department of External Affairs and has the
honor to refer to the Legation's communication to
the Right Honourable, The Prime Minister, dated
October 27, 1945, regarding the custody or Japanese
archives in the Union of South Africa. Reference is
also made to the Department's note dated December 4,
1945, regarding the handing over of the Japanese in-
terests in the Union by the Protecting Power. In
this connection it would be greatly appreciated if
the Department could supply the Legation with the
following information:
1. A brief description of the Japanese
archives in the Union of South Africa;
2.
An evaluation of the contents of
these archives.
In view of the language difficulty, the Lega-
tion appreciates that it may be difficult for the
Department to give a description and estimate of the
Japanese archives in the Union. It would be appre-
ciated, however, if the Department could supply the
Legation with any information which it may have on
the basis of the past activities of the members of
the Japanese staff in South Africa in addition to
information based on scrutiny of the archives them-
selves. Pending the detailed examination of these
archives, which the Department will doubtless
undertake, it would be helpful were the Legation
informed as to whether the Japanese representatives
were inaiscreet or careless, or whether the quantity
of documents indicates that few, if any, were des-
troyed. Such information would assist the United
States Government in determining whether it would be
worthwhile, on its part, to make arrangements for
an examination of these archives by a team of
examiners, and to assess the probable value of such
examination.
It is noted from the Department's reply to the
Legation's note of November 27, 1945, that the Union
Government is at present giving attention to the
matter
CONFI DENTI AL.

=== Page 229 === (confidence 0.98)
AIR MAIL
-2
matter of the handing over of Japanese interests
in the Union by the Protecting Power. If a de-
cision in this matter has been arrived at, the
Legation would appreciate being informed as to
what procedure will be followed in this regard.
The Legation desires to inform the Department
that the Department of State in Washington is
anxious that access to Japanese archives, crypto-
graphic material, etc., in the custody of the
Government of the Union of South Africa be granted
to members of the Legation staff for personal
examination, and that all information obtained
from the archives by the South African authorities
be shared with the Legation. It would be appre-
ciated, therefore, if the Department would also
inform the Legation of its plans in this respect.
C A P E T O  H,
February 28, 1946.

AIR MAIL CONFIDENTIAL:
Enclosure No. 2 to despatch no. l063, dated Septem-
ber 3, 1946, entitled "Japanese Archives in the
Union of South Africa" from the American Legation,
Pretoria.
UNION OF SOUTH AFRICA.
DEPARTMENT OF EXTERNAL AFFAIRS.
The Department of External Affairs presents
its compliments to the American Legation and, with
reference to the Legution's note of the 28th Febru-
ary, has the honour to state that the Union author-
ities have now completed their examination of the
archives of the former Japanese Legation in Pretoria
and Japanese Consulate in Cape Town.
The examination has failed to disclose any-
thing of interest.
The archives of the erstwhile
Legation were contained in one sealed trunk and three
packing cases which when opened were found to contain:
a)
 files of commercial correspondence, a perusal of
which revealed nothing of value;
b)
Statutes, Blue Books and other reference books
of the Union Government;
c)
bound volumes of laws, treaties, etc. in the
Japanese language, and dictionaries;
a)
cinematographic films which have not yet been
projected but appear to be travel scenes in
Japan.
The archives of the rormer Japanese Consulate
in Cape Town which were taken over from the Swedish
consular representative in that city consisted of
four wooden cases and one trunk. When opened it
was discovered that the trunk and cases were filled
with nothing but stationery and envelopes.
P R E T O R I A,
29th August, 1946.

Sir:
of July 12, 1946 and previous correspondence relating to
the examination of tne Japanese consular archives now
held by the Canadian Government.
The examination of the archives recovered from the
Japanese Consulate at Vancouver was made from August 19
to 23 by Mr. J. W. Amshey of the Department of State,
Lieutenant T. G. Tsukahira of the War Department, officers
of the Department of External Affairs, the R.C.M.P., the
R.C.N.R., the National Research Council, Lieutenant Colonel
Harold Richey, Assistant Military Attache, and an officer
of the Embassy.
The material consisted of official records and docu-
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ments, reference books in English and Japanese, motion
RELOTING SERICES
DIVISION DI TORIGN
SEP1946
picture equipment and film, flags, wall pictures, and office
C
S
supplies, and were contained in fifteen boxes, numbered 5
through i9, the contents of which are listed in detail in
enclosure No. 1 to this despatch.
The examination disclosed little of interest to the
Canadian or American authorities present. A cardboard box
of coded and clear text telegraphic messages was found,
but none were dated 1ater than December 1936 and the
subjects were not of interest. From box No. 6 were
extracted a number of items shown in Enclosure No. 2 as
of possible interest in the study of propaganda activities
of the Consulate, the control exercised by the Consulate
over Japanese residents in Canada through local associations,
and individuals under suspicion by the Japanese Government.
The last item on this list was withheld by the Department
of External Affairs as it "appears to be a matter of purel
domestic political concern and therefore may more properly
be dealt with by the appropriate Canadian authorities",
according to that Department. The remainder were carrina
by Mr. Amshey to Washington for microfilming, the Embas
undertaking to return the originals with two copies to the
Canadian Government after this work is completed.
No representatives of the British, Chinese, or Soviet
Governments participated in the examination; the offer
Hr-to
made
HH

=== Page 233 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
made by the Canadian Government to the United States
Government to share in the examination was not extended
to their representatives at Ottawa and the matter was not
raised with them by the Embassy in view of the Canadian
position.
Although the Embassy had previously been informed
by the Department of External Affairs that the archives
of the Consulate at Winnipeg would likewise be available
for examination at this time, a thorough search by the
Canadian authorities failed to disclose their whereabouts
and I was informed as a result by an officer of that
Department that their existence is "extremely doubtful".
As I have previously reported, the archives of the
Japanese Legation at Ottawa as well as the remaining
consular establishments were destroyed in their entirety
by the Japanese, according to the Canadian Government.
The office furniture and other property apart from
the records and archives and including a Government-owned
building at Vancouver was placed in the hands of the
Canadian Custodian of Enemy Property.It 1s understood
that sale of some of this property may have been effected,
the proceeds being placed in a special account.
Respectfully yours,
For the Ambassador:
Lewis Clark

All Govt Ministries Archives have been carefully
screened by Intelligence experts. Documents taken
from Jap Foreign Ministry are (REDEPTEL 484 September 16)
listed in shipping 11sts advice No. SA-15039 and SA-15060
sent to Washington Drcument Center. Greater East Asia
Ministry Archives were before surrender incorporated
with Foreign Ministry Archives. Many documents were
destroyed during the war or before arrival American
Forces. Greater part of Foreign Ministry documents have
been turned over to International Prosecution Section for
use as evidence in War Crimes trials. As they are re-
turned from Ips they are, with few exceptions, being
shipped to Washington Document Center.
Suggest Dept have careful study made of above-
mentioned lists of documents as they are bel1eved to
contain practically all significant material of interest,
including Ja? International Activities, which have not
been destroyed by Jap or lost during war. Dept may also
wish to consult general report on Govt Records Project
made by officer in charge of project to Chief of Washington
Document Center, advance dated March 31, 1946, a copy of
which sent Washington. Documents listed in shipping advices
Confidential File
but not yet available in Washington can in most instances
be microfilmed by General Hq and forwarded to Dept if
urgently desired.
Suggest question of sending special officer here
be postponed until study of shipping liats and documents

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
PROJECTS
SPECIAL/DIVISION
October 2, 1946
FC - Mr. Neal
In view of Mr. Bradford's request we have been glad
to eliminate the third paragraph of the draft airgram to
London. We are unable here to perceive either harm or
Files
Central
RM/R
advantage in the omission.
I am concerned at any implication, no matter how
inexplicit, that diplomatic and consular arohives
exist only for the purpose of intelligence and could
just as well be destroyed when that purpose is served.
I feel that the British attitude along those lines is
short-sighted and the result of lack of coordination
such as I have observed in British attitudes very often.
As a Foreign Service officer by vocation, I would not
like another government to look at our diplomatio and
consular archives from that point of view,nor would
4:14/10-246
the British As you know, the more Foreign Service
officers come to be regarded as intelligence agents,
Eav
the less effective they are likely to be. As a matter
of fact, such archives are in part history and in part
public records such as are maintained at courthouses
indicating the existence of individuals and their
property and civil rights. The value and implioations
of such papers are far wider than the single question
of intelligence and it is that wider concept as well
as the probable need of the archives as tools in re-
establishing foreign representation of Japan and
Germany through other means than the American Foreign
Service that SPD has been considering from time to time.
CS/W
As we have repeatedly indicated, we have no desire
to infringe on FC's intelligence prerogatives. We also
hope that our having legitimate interests of another
kind in the same questions or objects will not be
considered as treading on your toes.
Brci
3.1.
A. E. C.
OCT4 1946
SPD:AEClattenburg:BBS
S(B

I have the honor to refer to the Department's circular air-
gram 3610 of January 31, 1946, 12:30 p.m., requesting a brief
description and evaluation of the records and archives belonging
to the Japanese Consulate General in Wellington, and to previous
correspondence on this subject.
NC
On June 17, 19i6, a protocol was signed by the Swiss Consul
in Wellington and by the Secretary of Externel Affairs formally
transferring all Japanese property in New Zealand, which had been
in the custody of the Swiss Consul during the war, to the New
25r0
Zealand Government. In pursuance of the instructions contained
2
1JK
In the Department's airgram 1125 of June 12, 1946, the Legation
then addressed a request to the Ministry for an early opportunity
/丁A
to examine the Japanese archives; end on September 26, 1946, the
10℃
Legation was notified by the Ministry of External Affairs that
6ccD
it would be pleased to make facilities aveilable for an inspectinn
at any time convenient to the Legation.
1C16
2 ES
A preliminary inspection of the archives has now been made
by the Third Secretary of the Jegation (an O.N.I. Japanese trans-
I Wau
lator and interpretor during the war) and by Mr. CUNNINGHAM of
the Ministry of Externel Affairs (formerly with the Allied Trans-
1 Navy
lation and Information Service). These two officers concur in
1 FBP
the opinion that the Japanese archives probably contain no docu-
ments which would suggest that the Japanese Consulate General
engaged in anything but purely routine consuler affairs, although
it was noted that the Consulate General occasionally disseminated
SEP
in the form of books and pamphlets explanations of Japan'a"New
Order in East Asia" and in presenting the Japanese version of the
3
"China Incident". There were no cryptographic meterials.
2
The Legation has been informed that when the Wellington police
raided the Consulate General immediately following Pearl Harbor
they found the Acting Consul, Namio Nakafuji (浪 男藤)
completing the incineration of the Consulate's confidential files.
The Acting Consul appears to have carried out their destruction
carefully and thoroughly.
The archives consist of thirty-three wooden boxes encasing
CONFIDENTIAL
books,

Sir,
With reference to Your Excellency's letter of December 3, l945,
conoerning the request of His Majesty's Government in the United
Kingdom in concert with the Governments of the United States, China
and the Soviet Union, to be given an opportunity for their
representatives to examine any Japanese diplomatic and consular
archives, which have come or may come into the hands of the
Netherlands Government and in continuation of my letter of January
2, 1946, No. 24926, I have the honor to state the following for
Your Excellency's information.
The only Japanese archives which are at present in the hands
of the Netherlands Covernment are those of the former honorary
Japanese Consul in Curaçao. These archives have been examined by
the attorney-general in Curaçao, but nothing of any importance
has been found.
I avail myself of this opportunity to renew to Your Excellency
the assurance of my highest consideration.

My dear Ambassador,

=== Page 295 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to Despatch No. 2079 of December 10, 1946, from the
American Embassy, Bogotá, Colombia.
Legacion de la Republica de China
Habana, Cuba
December 26, 1946.
Mr. Gerald A. Molma
First Secretary
Embassy of the United States
Bogotá, Colombia.
Dear Mr. Mokma:
You will no doubt remember Mr. Tu, Chinese
Minister to Panama, who as our Ambassador attended the
recent Presidential inauguration in Bogotá. Mr. Tu
tells me that he had a conversation with you regarding
the archives and properties of the Japanese Legation
in Bogotá, and informed you that we were interested
In the matter because Doctor Li, Chinese Minister to
Cuba, is also accredited to Colombia. The last in-
formation we had about the matter was in December 1945,
when it was proposed that the archives and properties
mentioned above be turned over to the representatives
of the Four Powers, but we have had no further news
since that time. Our Foreign Office in Nanking has
now asked us to report further developments, and as
I understand that you are handling the matter for your
Embassy, I wonder if you would be kind enough to let
me know what has transpired since last December, so
that I may pass on the information to our Government.
Yours very truly,
(signed) Cheng Pai-Fong
Chargé d'Affaires ad-interim

=== Page 296 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to Despatch No. 2079 of December 10, 1946, from the
American Embassy, Bogotá, Colombia.
Bogotá, Colombia,
December 9, 1946.
Mr. Cheng Pai-Fong,
Chargé d'Affaires ad-interim,
Chinese Legation,
Habana, Cuba
Dear Mr. Cheng:
Your recent letter addressed to Mr. Mokma con-
cerning the archives and properties of the Japanese
Legation in Bogotá has come to my attention, in view
of r. Mokma's departure for a new post some time
ago.
I am happy to enclose copies of two despatches
of this Embassy to the Department of State reporting
the results of the two sales of Japanese Legation
property.
The archives of the Japanese Legation are being
held under the joint custodianship of the British
Embassy, the Soviet Legation and this Embassy. They
are not voluminous and they do not appear to contain
anything of great value. Full reports on the avail-
able information concerning them have been made to
the Department of State in Washington.
I trust that the information contained in the
enclosed despatches and in this letter will be of
assistance to you.
Very truly yours,
Aaron S. Brown
Second Secretary of Embassy
Enclosures:
Despatch No. 1556 of April 1, 1946.
Despatch o. 1956 of 0ctober 17, 1946.

Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: Jamuary 2, 1947
SUBJECT:
Interest in Establishing Jap anese Foreign Office Project
PARTICIPANTS:
RE - Dr. Noble
FC - Mr. Gosho
COPIES TO:
RE - Dr. Noble
894.414/1
ere
1-1408
Dr. Noble, Chief of RE, is interested in starting a Japanese Foreign
Office project and he is interested in finding out just what type of
247
Japanese Foreign Office Ministry archives are available here in Washington
and in Tokyo. In the case of the Japanese Foreign Ministry archives in
Tokyo, he would like to know how complete they are (e.g., if any had
been destroyed or if he can obtain diplomatic information as far back
as 19l5). He is also concerned as to the whereabouts and accessibility
0.02
of the Greater East Asia Ministry files, also the Office of the Prime
Minister.
I will start immediately on the inventory at Washington Document
Center and IAD and send a wire to Tokyo.

I have the honor to refer to Mr. Page's letter of Deeember
4, 1945, to Mr. Tsarapkin in which there was enelosed a sopy ef
an order insued by General MaeArthur to the Japanese Goveramest
on October £5 regarding the custody of Japanese diplomatie ané
consular property and arehives.
and China, have not discussed nor come to an agreement regarding
the rinal disposition of such properties and arehives. My Geverm-
mant feels that it would be helprul if the four prineipal Geverme
ments would consider this matter and come to an agresment regarding
it. It desires to proceed on a prinoiple of maximum latitude fer
the local representatives or the four Allied Powers in desiding upem
disposition of these properties and arohives.
Accordingly, my Government proposes, after obtaining the
concurrences or the Soviet, British, and Chinese Governments to
inform the Japanese Government through the Supreme Commander for
the Allied Powers, of the methods whioh the four Allied Powers
will pursue in dealing with Japanese properties and archives in
certain areas. I present below the proposed text of a note to the
Japanese Government:
"By agreement of the Governments of China
Great Britain, the United States and the Soviet
Union to whose representatives the Imperial
Japanese Government turned over its consular
and diplomatio arohives and property in pursuance
of a directive issued by the Supreme Commander
for the Allied Powers at Tokyo October 25, 1945,
the Imperial Japanese Government is informed that
the Allied Powers concerned will administer Japanese
arohives and diplomatic and consular property in the
countries inoluded in paragraphs A and C of the
direotive under reference as follows:
A. Arohives:
1. will in all instances be preserved intaot.
Cost, storage and preservation will be charged against
the Japanese Government, to be financed if possible
from Japanese offioial funds in the country where the
archives are located.
2. Shall include official doouments, consular
registers and correspondence pertaining to Japanese
nationals living abroad.
3. Shall not include stamps, seals, flags,
insignia, propaganda material and similar items, which
will be subjeot to destruction by agreement between the
four Allied Powers or representatives concerned.
4. Shall not inolude diplomatic or consular
libraries which ere official movable property.

=== Page 315 === (confidence 0.98)
B. Official properties of all sorts will be
administered by therfour Allied Powers or their
representatives concerned in a manner ealeulated
by them best to preserve the value of the properties
until decisions respecting final disposition or the
properties or preserved value thereof are reached
by International agreement.
6. Privately owned property of former diplomatie
and consular personnel will, for the present at least,
be kept intact exeept for the sale of portions rapidly
deteriorating in value, on conditions that the procends
of suoh sales will be kept in trust accounts.
D. The applioation of the foregoing principles
will be recommended by the four Allied Powers in
instances where other governments are holding Japanese
arohives and property. With respest to Japanese arohives
and property under the sole jurisdiction of China, the
United Kingdom, the Soviet Union, or the United States
the foregoing prinoiples shall be conformed to in the
discretion of the individual power coneerned."
I shall appreoiate receiving at your early eonvenience
the Soviet Covernment's reactions to this proposal.
Please acoept, xcellency, the renewed assurances of

=== Page 333 === (confidence 0.98)
COPY
TO
A-B - Mr. Benton
DATE: February 7, 1947
EUR - Mr. Matthews
NC
XXX
FE - Mr. Vincent
U - Mr. Acheson
XXIAXKXX:
From
PA - Mr. Russell
It is proposed that a series of volumes of Japanese official
papers be published with a view to clarifying the motivations
lying behind Japanese diplomacy relating to World War II. This
proposal is closely related to the German War Documents Project,
recently inaugurated under the direction of Dr. Raymond J. Sonta
Riles
Central
DC/R
and is a part of the larger Axis Var Documents undertaking.
The aim of the proposed Japanese project would be, as in the
case of the German publications program, to give a complete, accu-
rate, and scholarly account of Japanese diplomacy relating to
lorld !ar II. It is believed that it is even more important to
publish relevant Japanese documents than to publish comparable
German papers, for the reason that we know relatively less of
Japanese motivations in international relationships than we know
Be
of the German, thanks to the researches relating to German diplo-
Returned to
macy after orld War I. It is alsc relevant that the United States
was brought into liorld ar II by the direct action of the Japanese
Government.
The project should be undertaken ir fiscal 1948 in order, in
the shortest possible time, to provide the material for the educa-
ticn of public opinion on this important natter and also to anti-
cipato the time when the documents in cuestion may be returned to
CSBM
the Japanese Government after the restoration of self-government
in Japan. Three or four years will be required for the completion
of the work.
It is proposed that the United States undertake the project
on its own account. In the case of the German documents, we are
collaborating with the British, and it is anticipated that the
French will soon be admitted to joint participation. If the U.S.S.R.
makes application, it will presumably also be admitted. In the
case of the Japanese documents, the circumstances are somewhat
different. The United States has possession of the documents and
will be able to proceed alone without legal or other serious em-
894.414/2-747
barrassments. This will greatly facilitate the progress of the
work. it is presumed, however, that any other allied government
desiring to have access to the documents would be granted such
19
access.
offrce

=== Page 338 === (confidence 0.99)
RE - Mr. Noble
February 21, 1947
Le - C. B. Snow
Title to the Japanese Documents in Washington Document Center
CONFIDENTIAL
The Legal Adviser is informed that there is nothing in the
Intelligence Division, War Department G-2 files to indicate that
title to the captured Japanese documents at the Washington Document
Center does not rest exclusively with the United States. While
there was an informal working agreement with the Fritish regarding
joint participation in exploitation activities there is no record
of any binding agreement requiring thet the British or any other
power give concurrence prior to the public release of the material.
The only exception is the case of Japanese maps of the scale of
1:50,000 or larger or reproductions thereof. Such maps not re-
cuired and retained in the Archives of the Washington Document
Center should in accordance with commitments be destroyed or
returned to the Chinese.
The  ffice of the Legal Adviser is of the opinion that the
United Stries Government has title to the documents in question
and that they may be published without the consent of any foreign
power.

FROM :
RE - Mr. Noble
SUBJECT:
Regarding the proposed Japanese War Documents Project,
I suggest that a telegram be sent to SCAP in some such terms
as the following:
Department of State proposes publication under US
authority of series of volumes of Japanese diplomatic
and related papers with view to presenting complete
and accurate account of Japanese diplomacy relating to
World War II and enlightening Japanese, US and world
opinion regarding Japanese motivations in foreign
policy leading to and during War. Period covered should
extend to 1890's. Materials Washington Documents Center
insufficient. Similar project presently in operation
on German War Documents. Department will call in
outstanding scholars for task and expects to complete
work in three or four years. Project involves sending
approximately five professional staff to Japan and will
require assistance of SCAP in providing living accommo-
dations, also assistance SCAP microfilm team and several
SCAP non-professional assistants in handling documents.
Expect to send professional staff beginning next
September. Department is studying proposal that series
of volumes in original Japanese be published in Japan
as

CONFIDENTIAL
-2-, #67, March 22, 1947, from Tokyo.
C. The erchives of the Army Ministry yielded a greater
number of files, and although many were considered to be
of little importance, the essence of time precluded their
separation into documents of intelligence or historical
value and those of lesser value. These files relate to
military topography tactical plans and maneuvers, opere-
tions in the Pecific and in Chine, staff meetings,
histories of various divisions end regiments, etc., some
of which dated beok to l870. Documents cbtained from
these archives were evacuated to Washington Document
Center under shipping advices numbered 15079, 15069,
15029, and 15025 during February and Apr11 1946. These
shipments contained a total of 3,086 different items
or approximately l1,00o volumes.
D. Records relating to transportation and communica-
tions, natural resources of Japan, iron and steel pro-
duction, egriculturel experiments, and statistics,
munitions industries, foreign trade, etc., were removed
from the Arohives of the Commerce and Industry Ministry.
These documents were evacuated to Washington Document
Center under shipping advices numbered 15041, 15056
15048, 15033 and 15047 during March and April of 1946.
The total number of documents evacuated amounted to
1,003 volumes.
E. Removal of records perteining to labor, econcmics,
child welfare, education and religion, crimes, political
parties, social problems, housing, etc., from the Archives
of the Home. Welfare and Education Ministries was numbered
15032, 15040, 15042, 15045, 15055, 15058, 15059, 15062,
15067 and 15085. This shipment was evacuated to Washingtm
Document Center in February, March, April and May of 1946,
and consisted of 3951 volumes.
F. Archives are presently located in the respective
Japanese Ministries, Bureaus, etc., except that Japenese
Bank Archives end those of the Ministry of Finance ere
located in the Bank of Japan.

AIR MAIL
I
Establishment of Committee Representing American. British and
Chinese Missions. and Procedure Adopted.
Plans for taking in custody the official Japamése property in
Portugal were made in August 1945. Under that arrangement the
Portuguese Government agreed to lend its good offices to the transfer
of Japanese diplomatic and consular property, provided the Japanese
Government instructed its local Minister to relinquish this property to
the Allied Governments. A committee consisting of officers of the
American, British and Chinese Missions in Lisbon (The Soviet Union
having no representation here) was accordingly constituted.
Only on October 23, 1945, the text of General MacArthur's pro-
posed directive for the liquidation of Japanese missions abroad, to be
issued October 25, became available, and the appropriate instructions of
the Japanese Government to its Minister at Lisbon were not issued until
early in November. Final and clear instructions, according to the Japanese
Minister, were received only on November 12, and on the next day he called
at the Portuguese Foreign Office and announced the termination of his
mission. On the same day, November 13, the representatives of the three
Allied Missions hereafter referred to as the Committee, accompanied by
Mr. Sampaio Garrido of the Portuguese Foreign Office called at the
Chancery of the Japanese Legation, formally taking over from the
Japanese Minister. Guards were posted, the safes and archives were
sealed, and the Committee established its rules for meetings and con
sultations for joint action in the administration of the property. An
additbnal Amerioan member (Mr. Pardillo, a clerk of the American Embassy)
was added to the Committee for the routine work, and for contact with
Mr. Inoue and Mr. Komine, Counselor and the First Secretary of the
Japanese Legation, whom the Minister had designated as liaison in
checking the property.
Guarding the Premises.
On the first day, November 13, guards, provided by the European
Theater Command, U. S. Army, were posted in four establishments: the
Minister's residence, the Chancery (two floors in an apartment building),
the Military Attache's office, and the Naval Attache's office. On
later inspection it was found that the two latter establishments con
tained only office furniture and items without particular value or con
fidential character, other items having been either destroyed or con-
solidated in the Legation. Accordingly, the guards from these two
premises were withdrawn, and the rooms locked and sealed pending re-
moval of the property to the storage facilities arranged for  the
Committee.
Guards were maintained, under the orders of an officer of the
United States Army, at two establishments, namely the building ocoupied
as a residence by the Japanese Minister and the two floors used as the
Legation chancery, until a complete inventory of all property had been
taken and the property removed to the warehouse leased for storage
purposes. This was accomplished by stages, and terminated by the
beginning of February, 1946.
III
Archivee.
What purported to be the Legation's archives were found in one
steel
SECRET

=== Page 372 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to Despatch No. 1481 dated May 7, 1947, from the
American Embassy, Lisbon.
SECRET
AIR MAIL
- 2-
steel office cabinet. They contained only routine correspondenee. The
Japanese language documents were examined by a Japanese-speaking employee
of the Chinese Legation who reported that he found nothing of any im
terest whatever. No cryptographic material of any character, no accoumt=
ing books or records, no flags or insignia, emd no political documemts,
or even registers, were found. Questioned on this point, the Japanese
Minister said that everything of a confidential nature had been destreyed,
on instfuotions, "immediately after the Potsdam declaration". It may be
said that in the several weeks at their disposition the Japanese staff
were able to do a good job of cleaning up. The Committee, assisted by
other members of their respective Missions were constantly on the alert
for such documents as might come to light as the taking of inventory
proceeded, but with completely negative results.
In any event, the so-oalled archives, the seals, the brass door
plate, a collection of motion picture films and certain card indexes
(from which the more interesting items appear to have been eliminated)
were segregated from the other property and stored in the Amerioan
Embassy chancery pending final instructions as to their disposition.
IV
Japanese Filma.
Some 40 reels of Japanese films were found in various places in
the Japanese premises. The Committee arranged for a showing of all of
these films by the office of the United States Naval Attache, inviting
the Service Attaches, and other representatives of the British and
Chinese Missions, as well as of other Allied Missions in Lisbon to
attend the showings.
The films were found to be propaganda material, and it was the
unanimous opinion of the Committee and of the Service Attaches that
even those war films which it had been thought might be of some historical
or military value are, in fact, of no particular interest. As noted in
Section X the films nevertheless have been stored with the Japanese
archives, for such disposition as may later seem desirable. A list
of the titles is annexed to this report. (See Annex C.)
V
Japanese Representation in Portuguese Colonies.
Certain records pertaining to the former Japanese consulate at
Oporto, closed some time previously, were found in the chancery and
consolidated with the Legation archives. There appear to have been
no Japanese official establishment elsewhere in Portugal or Portuguese
possessions, except at two points in the Far East, at Timor and Macao.
The Committee was subsequently absolved of responsibility for
property at these two places, where it is understood a separate pro=
cedure has been established under direct instructions of the Allied
Governments.
VI
Official Jananese Funda.
An account was opened in the Bank of London and South America, Ltd.,
at Lisbon, in the name of the three Missions, to be operated over the
joint signature of the members of the Committee. Into this account was
deposited

The Japanese Minister requested that particular note be made of his
reservation in relinquishing the funds of the Yokohama Specie Bank, Ltd.
He said that according to the strict terms of his instructions these
funds were not "official property" and he requested that the three
Governments be informed that the amount in question should be credited
to the bank in question. The Committee, nevertheless, insisted on
taking these funds into custody.
The Committee was unable to obtain any accurate information as
to the origin of the funds found in the Japanese safes. It was es
tablished that the procedure had been to effect transfers from Switzerland
to Lisbon, and the greater part cf these funds appears to have been so
transferred during the early summer of 1945. The question arose whether
certain Red Cross funds, intended for the relief of Allied prisoners of
war in Japan, may have been included, but no evidence to this effect was
obtained.
In addition to the cash which was physically taken over, there were
three official Japanese bank accounts in Lisbon, with the following

VII
Accounting Procedure.
Annexed to this report is a statement of the accounts covering
Japanese funds, which is self-explanatory. The chief assets consisted
of (1) the cash and bank balances mentioned above, (2) an amount of
Escudos 4, 562,000 which had been retained by the former Japanese
Minister and his staff and which was turned over by him to the Committee
immediately before his repatriation, and (3) the proceeds from the sale
of furniture, motor cars, etc.
The chief disbursements were incurred in connection with the
repatriation of the Japanese group, including an advance of Escudos
624,418.40 to the American Embassy at Madrid to cover expenditures in-
curred on behalf of the Japanese group in Spain in the same connection.
As of the date of this report the balance held for the Japanese account
is Escudos 31,228,139.32, equivalent to $1,261,488.00 at the official
rate. The amount in dollars will be somewhat less if calculated at the
rate of transfer abroad, with bank charges, etc. deducted.
The principal operations of the account may be summarized in a

Vouchers have been obtained for each item of expenditure. No
salaries and maintenance costs of Japanese personnel were involved,
since advances of salary had been made, presumably under instructions
from Tokyo, before the Legation ceased functioning. The Committee had
no liability for expenditures for rent for the quarters of the Military
and Naval Attaches. Under the lease contract for the chancery quarters
there was a liability for one month's rent only, but the Committee was
able to find another tenant, and to vacate the premises in time to
avoid any payment in this connection. There was, however, a claim for
damages to this property which, after lengthy negotiations, was settled
by a lump sum payment in the amount of Escudos 17,000 ($688.00). Reference
to this matter will be made in a subsequent section of this report. Rent
was paid for the quarters which had been occupied by the Japanese Minister
as a residence until the end of January, 1946, the building being in use
by the Committee up to that time, for storage, taking of inventory, eto.
The only accounting item of pre-surrender transactions made avail-
able to the Committee concerns two advances of 200,000 escudos each
(total about $16,160.) made by the Japanese Minister, Morito Morishima,
to the Legation of Thailand (Siam) at Lisbon under date of April 5 and
July 19, 1945. Receipts for these advances, signed by Colonel Mom
Snidvongsseni, the Siamese Minister, were found in one of the safes and
were later transmitted to the Department of State at Washington for
adjustment of the account through other channels.
VIII
Loaaed Property.
The Japanese Government owns no real property in Portugal. The
situation as regards the various leases was as followss
1. Office of Naval Attache: The office quarters were combined
with the officer's living quarters under a personal lease contract.
All official property was inventoried and removed to warehouse; no rent
payments by Committee, and no outstanding obligations.
2. Office of Military Attache: Rent contract terminated under a
clause in the lease, as of November 30, 1945. Rent for November had
been paid in advance by the Japanese. All official property inventoried
and removed to warehouse; no outstanding liabilities.
3. Legation Chancery: Two floors in an apartment building. Term
ination of leases possible under contract, as of January 25, 1946. The
rent for December 1945 had been paid by the Japanese, and the rent for
the following month was paid by the Committee but later refunded by the
new tenant, who took occupangy of the quarters within the contract period.
These quarters had been rather badly damaged by the Japanese oocupancy,
and the landlord claimed an amount of 27,644.90 escudos to cover the
necessary repairs. It was found that the Japanese had deposited an
amount of 6,o00 escudos against such an eventuality, thus reduoing the
amount claimed by the landlord to 21,644e90 escudos. The Committee
agreed that the damage was in fact considerable, but felt that a part
of the redecoration should not be charged to the Japanese account.
Independent appraisals were made, and notwithstanding the fact that the
landlord presented receipted bills for his expenditures, the Committee
was able to reach a compromise settlement by a lump sum payment of
17,000 escudos (about $688.00).
4. Legation Residenge: The lease contract for this property provided
for

Enclosure No. 1 to Despatch No? 1481 dated May 7, 1947, from the
American Embassy, Lisbon.
