for termination as of the end of April 1946. Monthly rent, amounting to
6,400 esoudos (about $260.00) had been paid by the Japanese up to he
end of December 1945. The Japanese Minister vacated the premises two
or three days after terminating his mission, and the greater part ? the
Committee's work in examining archives, taking inventory, etc. was done
in these premises which remained under guard until January 23, 1946.
Arrangements were made with the proprietor for the termination of the
lease as of January 25, 1946. While the property had suffered some
damage during the Japanese occupancy, the landlord generously refrained
from presenting claims for damages.
Ix
Inventory.
A careful inventory was taken of all the official Japanese property
taken over by the Committee, with the exception of those items which, in
the opinion of the Japanese Minister, no record need be kept. The items
in question were the books, miscellaneous expendable office supplies, etc.
A copy of this inventory signed by the three members of ize Committee
was handed to the Japanese Minister, Mr. Morito Morishima, a the time
of his departure from Lisbon in January 1946. Copies of the nventory
have already been furnished to the Foreign Offices of the three Allied
Governments.
x
Disposition of Japanese Property.
Since it was desirable to vacate the Japanese property at the
earliest possible date and since the Japanese Governmeni owned no
buildings in Lisbon, it was necessary to arrange storage space in a
commercial warehouse pending the disposition of the property. The ex
penses incurred in this connection were very considerable, but even so
the storage rooms were such that the property would have depreciated con
siderably in value, because of moisture and rodents, if kept there for any
extended period of time. It had been the view of the Committee, heartily
endorsed by the Japanese Minister himself, that all of this property
should be disposed of by sale.
The British and Chinese representatives had received authorization
to arrange for the disposition of the property in this manner. The in
structions to the American representative were to the effect that the
Department of State preferred that the property remain in storage, with
the alternative authorization that "where storage of such property is
impractical due to excessive quantities or disproportionate expenses,
or the danger of depreciation or deterioration, sale may be made and the
proceeds thereof deposited in the joint trust account". After a care
ful survey of the situation, the Committee was unamimously of the opinion
that the property should be sold. The factors taken into consideratiom
in reaching this decision were: (1) the lack of any Japanese-owned build
ing for storage, as mentioned above, and the very considerable expense of
storage in a commercial warehouse; (2) uncertainty as to the time when
the Japanese Government mmy again be in a position to reestablish a
mission in Portugal and the probability that suoh a mission would in
any case be on a much smaller scale; (3) the fact that the property
appeared to have been bought locally, for purely utilitarian purposes,
and to have already deteriorated somewhat in use; (4) the complete lack
of any articles of historical or artistic value, in the opinion of the
Committee, and as specifically confirmed by the Japanese Minister himself;
and (5) the fact that at this juncture goods of this kind were in short
supply

=== Page 377 === (confidence 0.99)
sure No. 1 to Despatch No. 1481 dated May 7, 1947, from the
American Embassy, Lisbon.
SECRET
AIR MAIL
-7-
supply on the Lisbon market, and one could reasonably expect to obtain
high prices for them at public sale (as the event proved, many of these
articles were sold at prices higher than the general run of prices for
new articles on the local retail market at the time the articles had
been originally purchased).
It should be noted that nearly all of the furnishings in the
residence of the Japanese Minister were the property of the propriotor
of the building, and not of the Japanese Government.
The sale was by public auction, well advertised in advance and very
well attended. The receipts from the auction and from the sale of a
few articles sold to Allied diplomatic missions amounted to 467,166
escudos for the office and household furnishings, and 309,840 escudos
for the motor cars (7 passenger cars, 1 light truck, 1 motorcycle and 2
small trailers).
The archives, card indexes, films, an office copy of the final
account, to which the supporting vouchers have been attached, and a fow
other articles have been placed in one steel cabinet and stored in the
Chancery of the American Embassy.
XI
Repatriation of Japanese Officials.
In accordance with instructions received from the Department of
State in Washington arrangements were made for the repatriation of the
Japanese personnel to Japan in Jamuary, 1946, the party sailing on the
SS PLUS ULTRA from Barcelona. Only two Japanese nationals remained
behind. One of these was an elderly artist (painter), married to a
French woman, who had resided in Europe for some thirty years, and whose
repatriation the Committee did not consider it necessary, after due
consideration of the case, to insist upon. The other was a clerk of
the former Japanese Legation, who secreted himself, and was again
located only after the departure of the official group. This man,
Michio Shiohama, was later repatriated separately to Japan by way of the
United States.
There had been found in the Japanese premises a considerable amount
of personal luggage which had been left here by personnel who had served
in, or passed through, Lisbon in earlier years. These effects were duly
tagged for identification and were returned to Japan with the official
party.
The expenses incurred with the repatriation were not considerable,
since the Japanese officials were already in possession of funds paid to
them, as advance of salary, before the closing of the Japanese Legation.
The Committee paid, however, for the transportation to Barcelona of the
personal effects mentioned above and also advanced an amount of 624,418.40
escudos to Madrid in connection with the repatriation of a similar group
from Madrid, on the same ship, and for expenses of the whole party while
awaiting sailing. Against this, however, the Japanese Minister before
his departure handed over to the Committee an amount of 4, 562,000 escudos
out of the funds which he and his staff had held as pre-surrender salary
advances. In the receipts handed to the Japanese Minister on January 19,
1946, it was stated that out of the amount of 4, 562,000 escudos an amount
of 1,000,000 escudos would be set aside to pay the travel expenses of the
Madrid group or others, the balance to be held on deposit in the joint
account in the Bank of London and South America, Ltd. The remittances to
Madrid, as already mentioned, amounted to 624,418.40 escudos. The
Japanese
SECRET

=== Page 378 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure No. 1 to Despetch No. 1481 @ated May 7, 1947, from the
American Embassy, Lisbon.
SECRET
AIR MAIL
- 8-
Japanese Minister retained an amount of 645,000 escudos to defray
necessary expenses enroute for his group, and an additional 100,000
oscudos to cover expenditures for the Japanese from Spain who had no
foreign exchange. The last two items do not appear in the Committee's
accounts for the reason that they represent funds held by the Japanese
personnel prior to the taansfer of the official funds and property to
the Committee.
The repatriation group consisting of 45 persons in all left Lisbon
in two groups, one of 24 persons on January 17 and one of 21 persons on
January 19, travelling by the Lusitania Express on those dates to Bareelona.
This group included five women and eight children and six newspaper corres
pondents. A list of the repatriated personnel is annexed to this report.
(See Annex B.)
XII
Conclusion.
With the submission of this report the Committeo considers that its
work is finished, except for the disposition of the relatively important
cash amount held in the Committee's name in the Bank of London and South
America, Ltd. The Committee therefore asks that it be instructed by the
respective Missions as to the disposition of these funds and that it then
be discharged.
Annexed to this report is a detailed statement of the accounts of the
Committee. All expenditures are supported by vouchers whish have been ex
amined by the full Committee, and these vouchers, with a file copy of the
report, are being placed with the archives which are in storage in the
Chancery of the American Embassy. (See Annex A.)
The Committee as originally constituted consisted of:
For the Amerioan Embassy - Mr. Cavendish W. Cannon;
For the British Embassy - Mr. John Curle; and
For the Chinese Legation - Mr. Tien Feng-Chang.
During periods of Mr. Cannon's absence from Portugal on other details,
Mr. Halleck L. Rose and later Mr. Joseph Wagner served as his alternate.
After the departure from Lisbon of Mr. Tien, Mr. Nai-Tchun Liou and later
Mr. Hsien-Tseng Yang represented the Chinese Legation. After the departure
from Lisbon of Mr. John Curle, Mr. Nigel C. Trench served for the British
Embassy. The Committee also had the assistance of the experts of the re
spective missions in matters affecting security, SAFEHAVEN, and interpreter
service. Mr. Raymond Pardillo, clerk of the American Embassy, served through-
out the period of the Committee's activities as the Committee's general
administrative assistant.
Cayendish W. Camon
Nigel C. Trench
Hsim-sung Vancy
Hsien-Tseng Yang
Lisbon, Portugal
April 29, 1947
Annex

MINUTES OF THE MEETING OF THE ALLIED COMMITTEE ON JAPANESE
AFFAIRS IN PORTUGAL
Held in the Chancery of American Embassy on November 15, 1945.
The following were present at today's meeting:
Mr. Tien Feng-Chang,
Mir. Curle
Yr. Cannon and
Mr. Pardillo.
Mr. Cannon reported that the packages of banknotes found
in the Japanese Legation Chancery the previous day had been
checked again by eight bank tellers at the Bank of Portugal,
in the presence of a representative of the Committee and
2,000 escudos were missing, i.e. in one package supposed to
have 100 one-thousand escudo notes there were only 98. This
was one of the packages which was open, the others being
tied up with bands bearing the green wax seal of the Bank
of Portugal. It was agreed that nothing could be done
about the missing bills, since the first provisional count
at the Legation premises had of necessity been made in some
haste, and all reasonable precautions had been taken to keep
the money safe after the first count. It wes observed more-
over that on two items the Legation's statement of the accounts
had been found to be in error, one in excess and one in deficit,
and it was therefore agreed that the Legation cash item should
be reduced by 2,000 escudos.
The question of payment of miscellaneous bills was
raised, and it was agreed to draw a ci.eck for 20,000 escudos
for cash, to be kept by r. Cannon and r. Perdillo for
this purpose, Mr. ariillo vill keep an account of such
expenditures.
In connectior with the 400,000 escules, for which receipts
by the Siamese Legation were found in the Japanese Legation
Chancery, r. Cannon asked for information on the status of
the Siamese Legation, and ir. Surle stated that the British
imbassy had had instructions to the effect that the steps
similar to those taken concerning the Japanese Legation were
not to be taken in connectic it  Siamese Legation.
r. Cannon stated that the receirts ere for 200,000 escudos
advanced on april , 1045, and 200,000 escudos advanced on
July 19, 1945.
It was agreed, after discussion of the matter, that
the archives of the Japanese Legation should le assembled
into packares, sealed and rovision made for their storage
at sote safer place than the place to which the furniture
would be removed, to be deternined later.
The question of pe sonal effects left in the Legation
residence in suitcases was brought up, and the opinion was
expressed that it did not seem useful or necessary to make
a search of these effects. Mr. Cannon stated that in view
of the terms of taking over, the American representative on
the Committee could not insist on such a search, if the
Japanese  inister should object, until the receipt of
instructions on this point from the United States

Ir. agner opened the m eting by informing the Committee that a
reply had been received from the Department of State in Washington to

recommendations concernin" he isposition and liguidation of Japanese

1. The Committee noted the instruction of the Department of
tate to the effect tit sanction cannot be given to the destruction
of any of the Japanese arcnives nd erchives vill, therefore, be
retained in storage at the wmericen mba ssy chancery.
?. The Committee noted the epartment's instructions to Chingking
asking the merican Ambassador in that city, with the concurrence of his

regard to the taking over of Jupanese property other than official in
Macao nd  imor. It was agreed that the Chinese representative in Lisbon

3. The Committee noted the advice of the Department of State that
the advances mede to the Siamese Legation by the former Japanese Legation
cannot be settled in Lisbon nd that the receipts therefore be forwarded
to ashin ton. The Committee agreed that it ould be in order for Mr.
:agner to forward the receipts to ..ashin ton for settlement. In this
connection, ir. Curle mentioned that the question of the future financing
of the Siamese Legation in Lisbon is under consideration by his Government
and consequently should be considered es seperate and apart from the
preceding matter.
4. The Committee wes informed that the Department of State's in-
struction indicated that Japanese official property should, if possible,
be stored. The Department  lso advised, however, that in cases where
storage of such property is impracticable, due to excessive quantities,
dispreportionate expense or danger of depreciation or deterioration,
the property mey be sold and the amounts deposited in the joint trust
account. After discussin the matter thoroughly, the Committee agreed
that the Japanese property now in storage should be sold for the reasons
that the storage ch-rges are disproportionate to the value of the goods;
the cars are deteriorating, and their value constantly decreasing as
additional new cars become available, and that, in any event, the character
of the property now stored is not such as to warrant continued payment
of storage charges. The British and Chinese representatives have al-
ready obtained permission to dispose of official Japanese property and
the Committee as a whole has now agreed to dispose of the property on
hand by public auction.
In connection with the foregoing, the following exceptions were
agreed upon: (a) that each of the Missions involved could, if it so
wished, purchase one of the former Japanese automobiles at prices to be
established through appropriate valuation; (b) the supply of official
Japanese

Minutes No. 9
MINUTES OF THE MEETING OF THE ALLIED COMMITTEE ON JAPANESE AFFAIRS
IN PORTUGAL
Held in Chancery of Amerioan Embassy on April 29, 1947.
The following were present at the meetings
Mr. Trench
Mr. Hsien-Tseng Yang
Mr. Cannon
This meeting was called for an examination and discussion of
the draft of the final report of the Committee, the text of which
had been circulated for study some days previously. The draft was
approved, and the Committee then proceeded to an examination of the
accounts of the operations of Japanese funds for the period of November
1945 to April 1947, Mr. Pardillo, of the American Embassy, being called
in to lay before the Committee the vouchers supporting the various
items in the account. A draft statement of the account was examined
item by item, and the cash on hand in the petty cash account was
counted. Mr. Pardillo was instructed to deposit the balance in the
Committee's account in the Bank of London and South America, Ltd.,
and to proceed with the preparation of a clean text of the account
which will be circulated for approval, and attached to the final re
port, under the Committee's signature.
At the conclusion of its work the Committee approved a payment
of 8,o00 escudos to Mr. Pardillo as compensation for the administrative
and clerical work which he has performed for the Committee over the
period of eighteen months of the Committee's operation.
The Committee then adjourned sine die pending final instructions
from their respective Governments regarding the ultimate disposition
of the funds still held for the Japanese account.

=== Page 416 === (confidence 0.99)
ECONOMIC AND SCIENTIFIC SECTION
Labor Division
APO 500
31 July 1947
Mr. Philip B. Sullivan
Division of International Labor
Social and Health Affairs
Washington, D. C.
Dear Phil:
I am enclosing an advance copy of the Monthly Summary of labor
developments in Japan for June 1947.
As you no doubt know by this time, the Bill to establish the Labor
Ministry was introduced in the Diet a few days ago. There is every
prospect that it will be passed almost immediately, and the Cabinet
hopes to actually establish the Ministry on August toth. I have been
having a very busy time for the past few weeks working over the details
of the administrative organization and functions of the various bureaus,
as well as the proposed budget.
Introduction of the Bill was delayed because of two or three vigor-
504
ous contreversies between the Welfare Minister (Mr. Hitotsumatsu) and
the prospective Minister of Labor (Mr. Yonekubo). One of these was
concerned with Workmen's Compensation Insurance, which both Ministries
wanted. The decision was finally made by the Prime Minister himself
after conferences with Labor and Management representatives and the
7-3147
entire Workmen's Compensation program will be in the Labor Ministry.
Unemployment insurance, after a somewhat less vigorous debate was also
assigned to the Labor Ministry. There was a good deal of discussion
on the functions of Women's and Minors' Bureau of the Labor Ministry,
but it has finally been agreed that this Bureau will have broad respon-
sibility for coordinating women's problems in general, in addition to
those problems directly concerned with working women.
In general, we feel that the new Ministry is being given an adequate
charter which will permit it to assume an important place in the Japanese
Government.
I feel a good deal of satisfaction in the final introduction of the
Bill, as well as the way that these contreversial issues have been settled.
The gestation period has been several months longer than we anticipated,
and I feel that I have been pacing up and down in front of the maternity
ward and finally hear a thin wail to indicate that the birth has occurred.
I will refrain, however, from the obvious puns connected with the title
of the Ministry.
. vs cant

=== Page 417 === (confidence 0.99)
Detailed work on the budget of the Ministry has been a fascinating
experience, in view of the peculiar fiscal practices of the Japanese
Government and the current pressure for Government economy. I believe
we have succeeded in getting a budget which will permit the Ministry
to make a good beginning, although it wdll need supplementary appropria-
tions later on. (Incidently, I understand the U. S. Department of Labor
has had a few budget problems of its own).
As soon as there is an opportunity to get copies typed, I will
send you a copy of the Labor Ministry Law as well as the Ministerial
Ordinance which spells out the details of internal organization. We
have insisted on making both the Law and the Ordinance much more
specific than is customary in Japan--and while they may seem sketchy
by American standards, I believe they are quite satisfactory. There
are many other developments that I would like to write about, but
I will have to save them for another letter because a hot conference
is going on right now on the subject of unemployment insurance.
With personal regards,
Sincerely,
Paul h Stanohfild
PAUL L. STANCHFIELD
Deputy Chief

Ootober 17, 1947
REF - Mr. Ottemiller
CIA Memorandum, September 26, 1947
In accordance with your instructions I investigated the Department's
requirements with regard to the Japanese documents which the CI pro-
poses to dispose of according to the subject memorandum. I suggest,
therefore, the following reply to the CIA:
The captured and requisitioned Japanese records and docu-
ments which the Central Intelligence Agency proposes to trans-
fer to the National Archives are of potential interest to the
Department of State, particularly in connection with its work
in historical policy research.
The Department's requirement for future research in these
documents will be satisfied providing they are transferred to
the National Archives and the terms of transfer negotiated
between the Central Intelligence Agency and the National
Archives are not such as to preclude free access to the docu
ments by the Department of State.
The documents noted in the subject memorandum under items
(a) and (b) and identified as: (a) records of the Japanese War
Ministry, 1871-1942, and (b) records of the Japanese Naval
Ministry, 1868-1939, are of particular interest to this Depart-
ment. In the event that these documents are considered for
transfer to a Government agency other than the National Archives,
the Department wishes to be notified before such transfer is
finally negotiated to insure free access to the documents for
future research purposes.
REF:JHO:VMC

The Seoretary of State presents his compliments to
His Excellency the Ambassador of the Netherlands and has
the honor to refer to the Ambassador's note No. 5770 of
November 14, 1947 in regard to the desire of the Netherlands
Government Institute of War-Documentation to determine
whether any material on the history of the Netherlands East
Indies is contained among the Japanese documents now'in
storage under the supervision of the Washington Documents
Center.
The Department of State has ascertained that the
responsibilities of the Weshington Documents Center were
aseumed in December 1946 by the Foreign Documents Branch,
Central Intelligence Agency. It appears from an investiga-
tion conducted by the Foreign Documents Branch that almost
all of the documents received from the Headquarters SEATIC
at Singapore were forwarded to the Pacific Military Intel-
ligence Research Section (PACMIRS) at Camp Ritchie, Maryland
and that approximately ninety-five percent of the SEATIC
documents were destroyed by PACMIRS.
The Foreign Documents Branch 1s prepared to undertake
a survey should it be deemed desirable to determine whe ther
any of the doouments of interest to the Netherlands Govern-
ment Institute of War-Documentation are currently available
in this erea; the search for specific documents could un-
doubtedly be undertaken on the basis of the liats vhich it
is understood were compiled in Batavia.
Since it 1s contrary to policy to permit nationals
other than American citizens to have direct access to the
buildings and files of the Central Intelligence Agenoy it
would be necessary to transfer the documents located on
the bas1s

=== Page 438 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
the basis of any lists submitted by the Netherlands Govern-
ment Institute of War-Documentation or from an investigation
conducted by the Foreign Documents Branch to the Department
of State or the National Archives. However, it should be
pointed out that plans for the transfer of all remaining
SEATIC documents from the Foreign Documents Branch to the
National Archives are already under consideration and it is
possible that such transfer will have been accomplished by
the time an actual inspection will take place.
The Foreign Documents Branch points out that in the
letter of May 30, 1947 from the Netherlands Government
Institute of er-Documentation to the Central Intelligence
Agency there was set forth merely a general inquiry whether
thare was available "material which 1s of interest for the
history of the Netherlands and the Netherlands East Indies
during the second world war" and that there was no informa-
tion that the Institute was especially interested in SEATIC
documents. In view of this fact the response of the Foreign
Documents Branch did not contain the information that ninety-
five percent of the SEATIC documents had already been destroyed.

It is concluded that:
a. Rights in lit nd artistic works existing in J
on December 7, 194l, thich hv b oated as enemy property by the
Japanese Goverment and vic ve lucntly been sequestered or hav
otherwise been taken away frcn teir cmsrs without their consent mhould
be subject to restoration upan request therefor being made by the omer
within a reasonable period and in such manner as may be presoribed by the
Supreme Commander. Jupaneso wartime ordinences cancelling, suquestering
or otherwiee prejudicing the rights of Allied nationals in their oopy-
righted property ehould be revoked; and existing Japanese laws should
be amended where they are found to be in conflict with the conclusions of
this paper.
U.Sr
b. Rights in foreign-omed literary and artistie works
should be protected in Japan. SCAP ehouid sacure the permission of the
author for the use of his work. It is recognized, however, that, during
the ocoupation, the use of copyrighted material without the consent of
the author mey, because of ite importance to such objectives of the
Ocoupation as the economio and cultural development of the Japenese
people, justify the Supreme Commander in making specific exemptions. In
the latter instance, compensation should be made whemever feasible for
c. The rights of Japanese nationals domiciled in Japan in
their literary and artistic works which have not been vested or which
came into being for the first time since December 31, 1945 should be
recognized in Allied countries subject to the laws and regulations of
those countries.
It was agreed that these conclusions represent the present thinking
of P CC, that PCC representatives should maintain this position when the
SA NACC substitute paper for 388 is discussed in the Working Group, and
that it should be the position maintained by the State Department represen-
tative on REC.

As historians interested in the proservation and
eventual use of modern historical materials, we are natur-
ally concerned that no opportunity should be overlooked by
our goverment to arrange for the future publication, under
suitable auspices, of certain Japanese historical records.
This Department has learned with great interest of
you e forts to develop a Japanese War Documents Project,
pardllel  the current German project and, like the German,
under the uspices of the State Department. Some project
of this xid secns to us to be urgently needed. We believe
5

rerngin very fragmentary and uncertain, and therefore of
linited use, until the relevant documents are made available
to interested scholars. The present time affords our govern-
mert a unique opportunity to assure that such materials will
be published under reliablo auspices in the near future.
While unaware of the special problems that may be involved,
we wish to express our earnest hope that the present oppor-
tunity to be in this important work will not be neglected.

26 June 1947
MEMORANDUM FOR Special Assistant to the Secretary in Charge of Researoh
and Intelligence, Department of State
SUBJECT:
Netherlands request for CIG material
1. Enclosed is letter dated 30 May 1947 addressed to the Central
Intelligence Group by Drs L. de Jong, Acting Director of the Netherlands
State Institute of War Documentation, wherein inquiry is made as to
whether representatives of the Institute may examine and obtain foto-
copies of certain historical material at the Documents Branch, CIG.
2. It is considered best for CIG not to correspond directly with
the Netherlands Institute in this matter. The letter is therefore re-
ferred herewith for appropriate action in the light of the facts herein-
after stated.
3. In addition to large quanitites of captured Japanesedocuments
which contain information of intelligence value, the Documents Branch
has received many thousands of documents which are primarily of histori-
cal interest and have very little or no intelligence value. Only a small
portion of the historical documents have been processed and catalogued,
the great mass of them being stored in CIG warehouse facilities. Although
some of these documents may contain information which would be of interest
for the history of the Netherlands and the Netherlands East Indies, a
project of considerable magnitude would have to be undertaken in order
to locate the desired documents and comply with the request that they
be made available for examination and be fotocopied. Due to the heavy
workload on high priority intelligence matters, it would not be practi-
cable to initiate such a project.
"Signed"
CHARLES P. NICHOLAS
Colonel, G.S.C.
Deputy Assistant Director for
Collection and Dissemination

Washington, D. C.
Dear Sir,
Immediately after the liberation of Holland a State
Institute of War Documentation was set up by the Netherlands Government.
For the past two years this Institute has been engaged in collecting
all sorts of information, printed and unprinted, on the history of the
Netherlands during the second world war.
Recently representatives of the Institute have been given access
by the Washington State Department and the London Foreign Office to
the German Foreign Office records now stored in Berlin. Similarly the
Institute has been given access to the U. S. Berlin Document Centre.
Both the Foreign State Department Document Centre and the U. S.
Document Centre proved to contain many documents which wereof paramount
interest for our Institute and of which fotocopies will be made available
to us. We have undertaken not to publish or to allow to be published
any material acquired in this way without consent of the American and
British authorities.
We have been told that it is possible that the Washington
Document Centre may also contain material which is of interest for
the history of the Netherlands and the Netherlands East Indies during
the second world war.
I would therefore be very grateful if you could inform me whether
any such material has been received by the Washington Document Centre;
if so, whether it is still in your possession and whether it would be
desirable and possible that it should be examined by representatives
of our Institute and fotocopied on our behalf.
Yours most sincerely
/s/ Drs L. de Jong
COPY
Acting Director of the Netherlands
State Institute of War Documentation.

Statement to Minister Reuchlin re SEATIC Documents.
SUBJECT:
You will recall that Reuchlin, at his recent interview,
referred to a note from the Department in answer to a Netherlands
note regarding SEATIC documents. The Netherlands note had been
in regard to the desire of the Netherlands Government Institute
of War Documentation to determine whether any material on the
history of the Netherlands Indies is contained among the Japanese
documents now in storage under the supervision of the Washington
Documents Center. The Department of State note replied that
approximately 95 of the SEATIC documents was destroyed by Pacmirs.
States note said that "it is contrary to policy to permit nationals
other than American citizens to have direct access to the buildings
and files of the Central Intelligence Agency", where the documents
Piles
Central
DC/R
This Document Must Be Returned to
are located. However, the note said the U. S. Foreign Documents
Branch is prepared to undertake a survey to determine whether
documents of interest to the Netherlands are available if the
Netherlands would provide a list. The note added that plans are
underway to transfer all remaining SEATIC documents to the National
Archives (where, it is implied, the Dutch Government could inspect
the documents).
894.414/2-348
Mr. Bond has informed me of the following:
He has been told that the documents were destroyed by
Pacmirs pursuant to a JCS directive. He does not yet
know how this directive was issued. Lists exist for
part of the documents destroyed, the rest were
destroyed without listing. He has been given indica-
tion that some of the documents destroyed were of a
nature which the Dutch would probably have liked to
look at. It will be apparently six months before the
transfer to the National Archives is completed. Mr.
Bond is trying to obtain further information on the
unanswered questions.
It is recommended you inform Minister Reuchlin as
follows:
e are still ipvestigating the destruction of a portion
894.414/

DR

ence it will probably take some while to obtain full

UNIVERSITY OF CALIFORNIA

BERKELEY 4, CALIFORNIA
March 1, 1948
894.414/3-148
Mr. G. Bernard Noble
Chief of Division of Historical Policy Research
Jepartment of State
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Noble:
The War Department has recently inquired as to whether
I would be interested in going to Japan as a "visiting expert."
Of course, I am anxious to go and am planning as to how I might
best utilize what spare tine I might have.
Here at the University of California we now have eight
Ph.D. candidates who are able to use the Japanese language and
who are interested in working in the general field of the Japanese
background of World War II. Consequently, we have initiated a
co-operative research program, in order to make the most of our
time, personnel and historical materials. During my trip to Japan,
if it materializes, I hope to do some extensive buying of books
and also to obtain microfilm copies of some pertinent documentary
material.
In this connection I would like to learn what the pos-
sibilities are of gaining access to Japanese diplomatic documents
after World War I. I remember your interest in the subject (from
our conversation at Riverside in 1946), but I do not know what
you have decided to do in the way of making the materials avail-
able for research purposes. If you have not yet copied any of
C
S
the documenis, would it be possible to gain permission to do some
copying, at our own expense? Or would it be possible to co-operate
with the State Department on some such program?
any information which you could send me that would help
us in our research on the Japanese background of World War II
would be greatly appreciated.
894.414
Vith best regards,
Deemere. Browre
DIVISION OF HISTORICAL
POLICY RESEARCH
Delmer M. Brown

=== Page 475 === (confidence 0.99)
TO
: REF - Mr. Kemp
DATE: May 3, 1948.
SUBJECT:
RESTRIOTED
I refer to your call at Ds on April 29 when you left
for consideration the attached memorandum from the CIA,
dated April 12, 1948, regarding the disposition of docu-
ments from the former Japanese Embassy in Italy.
Ds has long maintained the position that arohives of
the consular and diplomatic Missions of enemy Powers should
be preserved intact on general principles of International
Custom and Usage. DS considers this particularly important
894.414
in the case of documents relating to individuals or the
business transactions of individuals in the area which the
particular Mission or Consulate was established to serve.
The list attached to the CIA memorandum shows a number
of items in the latter category, specifically items 11, 12,
13, 14, 19 and 23. Ds cons1ders that these should be
returned to Italy to be placed with the other Japanese
records and archives there stored. Accordingly, it is
5
suggested that CIA be requested to turn these items over to
the Department of State.
The other items appear to be more general in nature and
for the most part of no future use to anyone. However, with
a view to supporting the general principle outlined above,
DS would recommend that if CIA and the various branches of
the American military establishment have no further use for
them that they be shipped to Japan for delivery at the
discretion of SCAP to the appropriate section of the Japanese
Government.
Ds feels strongly that none of the items listed are
properly includable in the national archives of the United
States.
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Attachment:

=== Page 481 === (confidence 1.00)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
WASHINGTON
In reply refer to
RE
SECRET
My dear Mr. Sebald:
On my return from a three weeks' visit to the Pacific
coast, I found your letter of June 1 surveying the status of
our proposal to establish a Japanese War Documents Project.
I appreciate very much the careful analysis of the problem
which you gave me, and am very much encouraged by your state-
ment regarding the favorable view which General Willoughby
and G-2 apparently have regarding it.
There have been some developments in the situation here.
As you undoubtedly know, Congress reduced the amount of
appropriations requested by the Department, which resulted
in a reduction in force throughout practically all of the
Department. One result of this was that the eight positions
allocated to the Japanese Project by the Bureau of the Budget
could not be set up.
Another consequence is that we are proposing to reduce
the project to the smallest possible proportions. I am pro-
posing that we confine our efforts to securing a complete
documentary record of the Foreign Office files in microfilm
form, and that we send over to Tokyo a team consisting of
only two microfilm operators with one professional superviser.
The sole objective of this team would be to microfilm the
important and relevant documents of the Japanese Foreign Office
covering the period back perhaps to about i895. I should hope
that the professional superviser would be able to make a selec-
tion of documents which would enable the team to complete its
work within two, or certainly not more than three years.
We propose not to increase the size of this team next
year, thus keeping the burden on the occupation at a minimum.
The microfilms would be brought back to Washington and preserved
for such
William J. Sebald, Esquire,
Office of the United States Political
Adviser for Japan,

San Francisco, Calif.
Tokyo, June 1, 1948
CONFIDENTIAL
My dear Mr. Noble:
I regret that it has taken us so long to formulate this reply to
your undated last letter to me regarding the microfilm project. we
have been at pains to have a number of consultations on this subjeot
with various individuals in G-2 Section, and have also brought the
894.414/6-148
matter to the attention of Major General C. A. Willoughby, Assistant
Chief of Staff in charge of that Section. I shall attempt in the fol-
lowing to answer in sequence the points raised in the numbered para-
graphs of your letter.
As far as the IPS documents are concerned, a communication from
Civil Affairs Division, Department of the Army, in late March or early
April 1948 directed that, after processing in G-2 Section of this Head-
quarters, they be shipped to the Adjutant General, Department of the
Army, by whom they will be made available to other interested agencies
of the Government, presumably including the Department of State. In
view of the great bulk of the material in the Ministry of Foreign Af-
fairs you may find it desirable to obtain the desired IPS documents
from the Adjutant General in Washington and to microfilm them there.
We are inclined to believe that it would take the greater part of a
year and a good deal of microfilm equipment to handle the Foreign
Office project alone. For purposes of reference, it might be useful
to state that the IPS documentation fills about 500 boxes measuring
2 by 2 by 4 feet.
AUG
The possible secrecy with which microfilming can be done, or,
FILED
alternatively, placing copies at the disposal of the Allies, seems
to be a matter of some misgiving in this Headquarters. We suggested
IS
in a communication to G-2 Section on May 8 that copies of the document
microfilmed under your project could, subject to inter-departmental
screening if necessary, be made available in Washington to other Alliea
governments
VvL
DIVISION OF HISTORICAL
J1
G. Bernard Noble, Esquire
JON T: 1948
Chief, Division of Historical
Policy Research,
Department of State,
DEPARTMENT OF SINNE
Washington, D. C.
CS

=== Page 483 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
governments desiring them. In its reply G-2 Section, that is, Major
General Willoughby, stated that the local exploitation of doouments
and archives would not go unnoticed by other foreign nations and that
it was improbable that requests and withdrawals of documents for ex-
ploitation by microfilming would not eventually be detected by the vari-
ous Allied and neutral missions now in Japan, the reaction of whom or
of whose govermments would be unpredictable. In conclusion, G-2 stated
that the project would have to face the risk of reviving Russian and
other demands for access to Foreign Office documents. It is not be-
lieved worthwhile to discuss this point further until a final proposal
for the project is presented to the Chief of Staff for approval. This,
we feel, cannot be done until, after perusal of the material now at
your disposal, you have formulated your plans and presented them to us
in an instruotion.
Regarding your numbered paragraph 3, we cannot at the prement time
elucidate the guarantees that might be set up to prevent loss of import-
ant documents through the maliciousness of ill-disposed Foreign offiee
personnel. However, it is the feeling of G-2 personnel in contact with
the Foreign Office that there are at least some Japanese in the Documents
Section who can be depended upon for faithful service.
That G-2 is not in a position to afford any technical assistance
to the project has been confirmed by Major General Willoughby himself,
although he stated that at a later date it might be possible to help in
some respects. We have not yet sounded out the Signal Corps on the
possibility of their furnishing assistance, but it is most unlikely,
in view of the magnitude of the project and the constantly bemoaned
shortage of personnel in this Headquarters, that any assistance from
Signal Corps would be forthcoming.
In regard to the magnitude of the work, we have had an investiga-
tion made of the classification system used for Foreign office documents
and have obtained estimates of the number of pages included in each file
mentary classification table (enclosure No. 1) which should prove basic
in the execution of your project, since it should be possible through
the use of it to eliminate immediately a number of categories which are
2/
not germain to your objective. I am also sending you herewith a table
of Foreign Ministry files covering the Showa era as far as August 1943
(enclosure No. 2) which shows the number of shelves occupied by each
file, the length of shelves covered by each and the approximate number
of pages in each. On the basis of this material we have made a calcu-
lation of the number of pages that would probably interest you, elimin-
ating certain extraneous file categories pertaining to services to
individuals

=== Page 484 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3-
individuals and administrative matters. If file categories C, J, L. M,
N, 0 and Z are dropped from consideration, there remain about 4,ol5,000
pages to microfilm. On the basis of a forty-hour work week and the aver-
age microfilming rate of 200 pages per hour, it would apparently require
years with one machine, or one year working with 9 or 10 machines to miore
film this number of pages. of course, it is realized that you may not
wish to cover the entire Showa period, which began in l926. However, the
labor of culling from each file category the documents related to the tar-
get period, whatever it is to be, will be considerable.
In reference to your numbered paragraph 5, on reconsideration I
see no reason why the basic instruction on this matter should not come
straight to us inatead of through Civil Affairs Division, Department of
the Army. On talking with General Willoughby, I found that he appeared
to be entirely sympathetic to the project in principle and that he falt
that there was no reason why G-2 should not fully concur in it. I  thore-
fore encouraged to believe that we will have the cooperation of G-2 in
executing your instructions.
I note that you state the quota of individuals to be sent over will
be exceedingly small. on the basis of enclosures Nos. 1 and 2 hereto, the
calculations set forth above, and those which you are now able to make
yourself, I hope that you will shortly be able to arrive at a definite
figure for the personnel requirements involved. G-2 points out in its
reply (enclosure No. 4) to our communication of May 8 (enclosure No. 3)
that there will arise many logistic and personnel problems of concern to
other sections of Headquarters. It cannot be overemphasized that these
problems will be taken quite seriously by Headquarters in view of the
stringency of billeting space, the general shortage of personnel, and the
necessity of forecasting commissary requirements precisely. It is there-
fore believed necessary to arrive as soon as possible at a fairly defin-
ite idea of the size of the team which will be sent here. we require this
information before presenting the whole matter to Chief of Staff for
approval.
In conneetion with your numbered paragraph 9, it would appear most
advisable to place the project under the official auspices of G-2 section.
Such action, it is believed, would tend to maximize the assistance that
might be obtained from G-2 and would not prevent whatever special super-
vision or direction it might be necessary for us to give the project.
While awaiting your decisions on this matter, I recommend that,
if the project is to materialize, necessary instructions be issued at
the earliest possible date, as some time may be involved in pushing the matter
through the Chief of Staff's office.
Very truly yours,
W. J. Sebald
Enclosures:

SAN SALVADOR.
Reference the Embassy's airgram A-158 of June 8, 1948
inquiring with regard to the ultimate disposition of the Germn
and Japanese archives in the custody of the Embassy.
The Department is gratified at the Embassy's efforts to
preserve the archives and appreciates the problem presented by
lack of storage space. The fact that a certain measure of deteriora
tion will result from the absence of adequate storage facilities
is accepted as inevitable by the Department. Nonetheless, the
Department considers the principle of preservation important'and
desires to see it served as well as possible. To date no decision
has been reached concerning the ultimate disposition of the archives.
Consequently, the Embassy is requested tc continue to maintain them
on the premises.
The Embassy may be sure that it will be informed as soon as
any decision is reached regarding the ultimate disposition to be
made of the archives.
MARSHALL
Mashill

-2-
6. If we add a sentence such as in my preceding para
we should also add an appropriate recommendation.
APPENDIX "B":
2. I think we should spell out what kind of administrati
problems we are facing, e.g., three powers in a four-power trust
ship are helpless when the fourth has pulled out without relinqut
ing her role in the trusteeship.
3. I don't think we should, or could, conceal from the
SANACC the identity of "this American Republic", although I
agree that we should not name it in the nates.
4. This is a conclusion rather than a discussion of the
problem. If the paper were read in the proper sequence, i.e.,
the Discussion before the Conclusions and Befommendations, it
would be well to retain this paragraph 4,/SANACC papers are
practically never read that way. As a matter of common practice,
these papers are written with the presumption that the reader
will see the conclusions before the discussion.
7. I am afraid that at this stage it is no longer clear
what is "customary", especially with regard to "properties of
this nature". The customary procedure for German properties
of this nature has been to liquidate them, even in countries
which could not claim them as successors to the former Gemman
Government. .That is exactly what we do not want these countries
to do with Jap diplomatic properties. So why not express the
hope that the properties and archives will be preserved pending
future international agreement as to their disposition.
DRAFT NOTE
The comments under 7, above, would also apply to the
corresponding passage of the draft note, and the term United
Nations country should be circumscribed so as to exclude the
neutrals, Siam and, in the unlikely event of their admission
to the UN, the Balkan satellites.
Otherwise, the paper is fine.

THE LIBRARIAN OF CONGRESS

WASHINGTON 25. D.C.

Sir:
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
We have learned on inquiry that the Department of State is
not obtaining microfilms of the records of the Japanese Foreign
Office of a type similar to those filmed from the Foreign Offi
files in Germany. Because we believe that materials of this type
will be of significance for future studies in this country, the
Library of Congress would like to suggest that film copies of such
4/10-2048
records be procured by the Department of State for the collections
of the Library of Congress.
The Library of Congres: is prepared to transfer to the Depart-
ment of State a working fund of approximately 30,000 more or less,
as well as the use of up to 3 portable microfilm cameras in order to
effect this objective. We would expect that as the film is produoed
it would be examined by the State Department and that auch portions
of it as can be made available immediately will be transferred to the
Library of Congress for possible sale to universities, research
institutions, etc., in the form of microfilm positives. The Library
of Congress is prepared to place restricted portions of the films
received from the State Department under such appropriate security
control and restrictions as may be laid down by the State Department.
If this proposal is acceptable, the Photoduplication Service of the
Library of Congress is prepared to provide technical specifications
for the production of these films.
Sincerely yours,
Luter Aimaus
hE.
Luther H. Evans
Librarian of Congress

My dear Dr. Noble:
I have received your letter of December 27, 1948 regarding the
documents microfilming project. As you have noted, my telegrams
no. 286 of December 11, 1948 and CS-66235 of December 12, 1948 were
drafted on the basis of the approval here of the project outlined
in the copy of the staff study which was enclosed with my letter to
Coatrol
you of November 30, 1948.
By now you have received my telegrams informing you that both
Mr. SHAW and Mr. SMITH will be completely acceptable here. For your
confidential information with regard to Mr. Delmer BROWN, it appears
that his inacceptability to the military authorities is related to
statements ascribed to him by the press upon his last return to the
United States, whether or not he was correctly quoted.
We have checked on the Library of Congress microfilm machine
which is now in Japan. It was loaned by the Librery to the National
Research Council of Japan for the use of the Council, in exchange for
CS/
which the Council undertook to do some microfilming for the library.
The machine's availability for use on your documents project would
seem to depend upon the amount of work which Mr. CLAPp of the Librory
DIVISION HISTORICAL
EP TATE
of Congress requests the Council to do for him. In the event that hi
requirements are not too great, the machine will probably be available
for your project at least one third of the time, perhaps more. At
present it is not possible to obtain a more definite commitment come
cerning the availability of the machine, and I would suggest your am-
sulting with Mr. Clapp.
I cannot think of any aspects of the matter which have not now
been covered as well as possible in advance. Upon the arrival of
SECRET FILE
the project team in Japan you may be assured of the wholehearted
cooperation of this Office.
Sincerely yours,

United States Political Adviser

Tokyo, February 8, 1949
UNCLASSIFIED
894.414/2-849
594.414
Dr. G. Bernard Noble,
Chief, Division of
Historical Policy Research,
Department of State,
Washington.
My dear Dr. Noble:
Your letter of January 19, 1949 was received here today.
I am afraid you have been given somewhat faulty informa-
tion concerning the housing situation. Although there is a
certain limited amount of housing furnished to the various
diplomatic missions in Japan of countries represented on the
Far Eastern Commission which the missions can assign to their
CS/C
personnel as they see fit, this is, I believe, the only such
housing which might be termed "diplomatic." As you may be
aware, our office here is not considered.a diplomatic mission
but a section of General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for
the Allied Powers, and as such it does not receive housing
under the foregoing syst em; instead, the personnel of this
office receive housing directly from General Headquarters in
the same manner as other personnel in General Headquarters.
I am therefore at a loss to understand the advice given you
by the Department of the Army concerning "diplomatic" housing.
894.414
I would suggest that you discuss with the Department of
the Army
UNCLASSIFIED
2
1
27854
8
f

=== Page 519 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Tokyo, February 8, 1949
the Army the possibility of having your working personnel come
out on Army transportation and, upon arrival, putting their
names on the housing list in the usual way, and bringing their
wives out on Army transportation when their turn comes on the
list. While I appreciate the fact that objections may be made
to this procedure in view of the temporary nature of the assign-
ment, perhaps the fact that it will last a year or more will
have a bearing. At any rate, I know of no other way in which
it might be done. Even if the Department were willing to send
the personnel and their wives by commercial transportation,
housing is made available only in the manner described, and it
would be necessary for the wives to await their turn in the
States for the assignment of such housing.
The waiting time on the housing list varies according to
the immediate demand and al so according to a complicated system
of "credits" which an individual is given on the list (number
of months overseas, date of marriage, number of months separated
from wife, etc.). At present the minimum wait is three months
and the maximum from seven to nine months.
With regard to Dr. Smith, I presume that by now you have
received my telegram no. 13 of January 13 and my letter of
January 21, 1949.
Sincerely yours,
W. J. Sebald
UNCLASSIFIED

Brussels Belgium, July 19, 1949
CONFIDENTIAL
Subject: Microfilm copies of the archives of the former
FCWE JA
Japanese Embassy in Brussels.
The Charge d'Affaire a.i. has the honor to refer to the
Department's Airgram No. 130 of April 25, 1946 and its telegram
No. 524 of May 20, 1940, on the subject of the archives of the
former Japanese Embassy in Brussels.
During the last two weeks of March, 1947 personnel of the
Headquarters of the 7707th Military Intelligence Service Center,
U.S. Army, Ai0 757, microfilmed these archives, consisting of
correspondence and documents, including maps and marine charts.
However, the Embassy is not in possession of negatives or
reproductions of the material copied.
On June 17, 1949, Mr. Nikolai Nikolaevitch Skobelev, First
Secretary of the Russian Embassy in Brussels, who was approached
for the purpose of obtaining his approval for the sale of
additional furniture of the former Japanese Embassy in order to
cover current and future custodial charges, mentioned that this
Embassy had failec to carry out its promise, given in its note
to the Russian Embassy of March 11, 1947, to submit microfilmed
copies of the archives of the former Japanese Embassy. A
similar promise was made to the other custodians, the British
and Chinese Embassies.
In order that this Mission may carry out these promises,
it would be appreciated if the Department would forward four
sets of copies, three to be transmitted to the Russian, British
and Chinese Embassies, one to be retained in the Embassy's
files.

My dear Dr. Evans:
I refer to the exchange of correspondence between
the Librery and the Department dated October 20, 1948
and November 5, 1948, pursuant to which the Library of
Congress established in the Department of State a
working fund of $30,000.00 "to f1nanee the proourement
of microfilm copies of certain records of the Japanese
Foreign Offioe". In accordance with the understandings
894.414/8-1649
embodied in the foregoing correspondenee, this projeet
was initiated in Tokyo in April 1949.. Microfilm rells
are now being recelved by the Department and, according
to the terms of the original agreement, they will be
transferred to the custody of the Library of Congress,
following the necessary security examination by the
Department.
With the assistanoe of the Library, the Department
of the Army, and ScAp, the Department of State has been
able to confine the expenditures of this project to a
minimum. Cameras have been loaned by the Library,
transportation of personnel has been furnished by the
Army, and SCAp has greatly assisted by providing suit-
able accommodations in Tokyo as well as in seouring
(at no cost to the projeot) ten Japanese technicians
to assiat in the filming and related operations. The
principal expenditures obligated by the Department,
therefore, have been for (1) two profossionals (P-7
and P-4) to direct the operation, and (2) the purchase
of microfilm rolls. As a result of these economies,
it will have been possible to finance the project from
Apr11 1949 to June 30, 1950 at an anticipated cost of
$25,271.60, leaving a balance in the working fund of
$4,728.40.
894414
In accordance
The Honorable
Luther H. Evans,
U
Librarian of Congress,
Wasnington 25, D. C.

SUBJ CT:
Clearanee of Mierofilm Polls of Japanese Foreign Offise
Documonts to be Deposited in the Library of Congress.
The Department of State has presently in operation in
okyo a project for the microfilming of the importent files
from the Japanese Foreign Office covering the period 1967
to V-J Day. This operation is being supported by funds
provided by the Li rary of Congress. Technioally, the under
894.414/8-2549
taking is a SCaP project which is providing a mumber of
Japanese technicians to assist the two professionals sent
over by the Department. The project was begun during April
of this year and slightly over l00 rolls of microfilm have
already been receivod.
The Department, in return for the support of the Library
of Congress, undertook to turn tho films over to that ageney
on the understanding that such safeg uaris would be esta lished
as tho Department might prosoribe.
The question now arises as to what vestrictions, if eny,
should e placed on the films that are sent to the Library
of Congress, whather, that is, the Library of Congress may
be permit:ed to make these films available to research
scholars under such conditions as the Library may prescribe.
In this connection it shoul be pointed out thet in the
cese of the German Foreiem Cffice rchives Project (Anglo-
Franco-imericen undertakins for the publicetion of a series
of volumes) it is agreed that vien a volume for & particular
CS/
poriod has been published, all the documents covering that
period shall be made available to responsible researchers.
S/M
This rule preveils down to the outhreek of Fcrld War I, the
understanding being that this particular aspect of the problem
will be surveyed in due course.
8
6
19
Recommendation:
M
It is recommended that as the microfilm rolls are received
from the Tokyo operation they b made aveilable to the Library
of

FE concurs in the recommendation that as the microfilm rolls are received from
Tokyo they be made available to the Library of Congress without restrictions down
to the outbreak of war with Japan, December 7, 194l, and that the question of
freeing the later materials from restriction be reviewed subsequently.
In this connection, it is assumed, as indicated in your memorandum of
September 2 to Mrs. Dunning, that it will be the policy of the Library of Congress
to make these materials available to all responsible parties, including other
are unrestricted.
It is also suggested that RE may desire to initiate in the near future a
review as to declassification of those films covering the war years.

THE HOOVER INSTITUTE
AND LIBRARY
ON WAR, REVOLUTION, AND PEACE
Stanford University, California
H. H. FISHER. Cbairman
C. EASTON ROTHWELL. Vice-Cbairman
September 22, 1949
PHILIP T. McLEAN. Librarian
PERSONAL
Dear Francis:
I know you are aware that we are interested here at the Hoover
in obtaining as much as possible of the Foreign Office documentation
of the former enemy countries. We were able to get a good déal of that
emanating from the German Office, both through our own collectors and
through the generous cooperation of Bernard Noble and Ray Sontag who
have seen to it that we obtained those things which were not restricted.
894.414/9-2249
There are additional materials from the German source of which
we have been informed and which pertain both to the interwar years and
to the years preceding World War I. We have had some very tentative
discussions about the possibility of microfilming these materials
and bringing them here for purposes of historical research. Since Ray
Sontag has left and Bernadotte Schmidt has taken over, there appears to
be some possibility of a change in policy and greater reluctance to
release such materials to a private institution. I am going to write to
Bernadotte to find out what that situation is, but I did want to inform
X.A 862.414
you of our interest in the materials.
Meanwhile, however, a more immediate problem has arisen with
respect to the comparable Japanese materials. Although the project for
microfilming these documents has been kept more or less under wraps,
we learned of it last year and Dr. Tom Smith, who is a member of the
Stanford History Department and who has also worked with us, is in
Tokyo aavising the job. Apparently these documents have been discovered
to have an unusually rich yield of vitally important historical information.
Some arrangements were worked out under which it was possible for us to
obtain segments of the microfilm materials. On the other hand, I under-
stand that the whole set is being microfilmed, and I am anxious to find
out whether there would be any possibility of our obtaining a more
complete set, excluding, of course, those that for various reasons might
have to be classified.
This is a purely exploratory letter, and I have sent it to you
personally and informally for the purpose of getting your advice as to
whether such an arrangement could be made and if so, how I should go
about it.
There are these arguments in favor of our getting as complete
a set as possible. In the first place, we now have a very competent
CS/H

=== Page 553 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
curator of our Japanese materials, Mr. Nobutaka Ike, an American-born
Japanese who served with one of the Army Language Schools during the
war and who is considered one of the most promising young scholars
in this field. In the second place, Tom Smith will return to Stanford
after completing his present task and will wish to press forward with
his own research in the field. If copies of the documents, the
organization and photographing of which he himself has supervised,
were available here, it would greatly facilitate his research and the
preparation of memoranda which might be useful to the Department.
The arguments on the other side will be best known to you.
I should greatly appreciate any suggestions you can make and
will be most grateful to get them.
With best wishes,
Sincerely yours,
Gast
Easton Rothwell
Vice-Chairman
ER)fs
Mr. Francis Russell, Director
Office of Public Affairs
Department of State
Washington 25, D. C.

DIVISION OF
FROM : RE - Mr. Humphrey
HEAST ASAN AFFAIRS
1949
SUBJECT:
WARNEN OF STATF
894.414/2-747
I attach a copy of a memorandum dated February 7, 1947 which
set forth the Division's objectives, as of that time, in proposing to
publish records of the Japanese Foreign Ministry. This proposal
was approved by or for Mr. Benson, Mr. Matthews, Mr. Vincent, and
Mr. Acheson.
Owing largely to the inability to secure appropriations within
the Department, the project was subsequently modified to cover only
microfilming of the records and not their publication by the Depart-
894.414/9-3049
ment. Funds were secured from the Librarian of Congress to under-
take the microfilming, and the Department agreed to deposit in the
Library of Congress such films as should not be retained for security
reasons. It is the intention of the Library of Congress to make the
films it secures from us available to the research scholars of
this country.
The microfilming project has been in operation since April
1949.
Attachment:
As stated.
